Simplicial lists in operad theory I
Redi Haderi, Özgün Ünlü

Abstract
We define a category 𝖫𝗂𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{List} whose objects are sets and morphisms are mappings which assign to an element in the domain an ordered sequence (list) of elements in the codomain. We introduce and study a category of simplicial objects 𝗌𝖫𝗂𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{sList} whose objects are functors Δ o ​ p → 𝖫𝗂𝗌𝗍 \Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{List} , which we call simplicial lists, and morphisms are natural transformations which have functions as components. We demonstrate that 𝗌𝖫𝗂𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{sList} supports the combinatorics of (non-symmetric) operads by constructing a fully-faithful nerve functor N l : 𝖮𝗉 → 𝗌𝖫𝗂𝗌𝗍 N^{l}:\mathsf{Op}\to\mathsf{sList} from the category of operads. This leads to a reasonable model for the theory of non-symmetric ∞ \infty -operads. We also demonstrate that 𝗌𝖫𝗂𝗌𝗍 \mathsf{sList} has the structure of a presheaf category. In particular, we study a subcategory 𝗌𝖫𝗂𝗌𝗍 Op \mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}} of operadic simplicial lists, in which the nerve functor takes values. The latter category is also a presheaf category over a base whose objects may be interpreted as leveled trees. We construct a coherent nerve functor which outputs an ∞ \infty -operad for each operad enriched in Kan complexes. We also define homology groups of simplicial lists and study first properties.



1
Introduction and summary

This paper presents a new approach to the combinatorics of non-symmetric operads. We propose a simplicial framework via simplicial lists. First, we define a convenient category of sets, denoted
\mathsf{List}
, whose morphisms are mappings which assign to an element in the domain a sequence of elements in the codomain. A simplicial list is a functor
\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{List}
.

A central contention of ours is that, in a strong sense, (certain) simplicial lists are to non-symmetric operads what simplicial sets are to categories.
Many of the constructions and theorems in this paper provide justification for this contention. For example, we construct a nerve functor and prove a nerve theorem which leads us to a notion of
quasi-operad
.

The intuition underlying most of our constructions and results is deceptively simple. Thus, in order to make the latter more technical sections more digestible, we wish to discuss some preliminary ideas in this introduction. We begin by a short discussion on the notion of operad and
\infty
-operad.

A few words on operads and
\infty
-operads

A non-symmetric
operad

\mathscr{P}
is comprised of

-

Objects
a,b,c\dots
, traditionally referred to as
colors
.

-

Multimorphisms, which have a codomain and, unlike categorical morphisms, have a sequence of colors serving as domain. Such multimorphisms are referred to as
operations
and are typically depicted as corollas

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}

to indicate that
f
has as domain the sequence
{\underline{a}}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
and codomain
b
. We may also write
f:{\underline{a}}\to b
.

-

Composition
of operations when placing operations on top of the inputs of another operation operation. We may depict such a composition scheme as a tree

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}^{(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n_{1}}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}

This tree encodes the data for operations
(f_{i}:{\underline{a}}^{(i)}\to b_{i})_{i}
and
g:{\underline{b}}\to c
. The composite of these operations is an operation
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{k}):{\underline{a}}^{(1)}*\dots*{\underline{a}}^{(k)}\to c
with domain the concatenation of the sequences on the top of the tree. Composition is required to be associative.

-

A
identity
operation
1_{a}:a\to a
for each color
a
such that composition is
unital
.

In order to abbreviate terminology, throughout this paper, we refer to non-symmetric operads simply as operads.

It is evident from the above sketch that, although operads, by virtue of having operations which compose, are objects of study in category theory, the theory of operads extends the theory of categories. Another feature is hinted at by the string diagrammatic style of depicting operations: operads generalize monoidal categories. In fact, any monoidal category
(\mathscr{V},\otimes)
may be regarded as an operad, typically denoted
\mathscr{V}^{\otimes}
, with objects those of
\mathscr{V}
and operations morphisms in
\mathscr{V}
of the form
f:A_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes A_{k}\to B
.

A morphism of operads
\phi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
, which is just a structure preserving map, is also interpreted a
\mathscr{Q}
-valued
algebra
of
\mathscr{P}
. Interpreting morphisms as algebras is most useful when
\mathscr{Q}=V^{\otimes}
originates from some monoidal category, the classical example being the category of sets with the cartesian product
\mathscr{Q}=\mathsf{Set}^{\times}
.
An operad
\mathscr{P}
is only as interesting as its algebras. A vast array of mathematical structures can be encoded as algebras over operads and we refer the reader to textbook accounts such as
[
30
]
,
[
35
]
or
[
31
]
for examples from multiple areas of mathematics and beyond. The original formulation of operads is due to Peter May
[
36
]
in the context of loop spaces.

The terminal operad
\mathsf{Assoc}
, called the
associative operad
, which consists of a single color and a unique operation with
k
-inputs for each
k\geq 0
, is particularly well-known. Its algebras
\mathsf{Assoc}\to\mathsf{Set}^{\times}
are monoids and more generally its algebras in a monoidal category are monoid objects in that monoidal category. Thus, the operad
\mathsf{Assoc}
may be regarded as a structure which records the notion of monoid.

If we pursue this line of thought, then as soon as we try to implement these ideas in higher dimension we run out of structure. For instance,
we want to say monoidal categories are
\mathsf{Assoc}
-algebras in
\mathsf{Cat}^{\times}
, but this would only cover strict monoidal structures. This observation would lead us to consider weak 2-morphisms
\mathsf{Assoc}\to\mathsf{Cat}^{\times}
and implement ideas from 2-category theory (a couple of examples being
[
23
]
,
[
15
]
). Soon enough though, we would need even higher morphisms and we run into the usual problem of inability to properly keep track of coherence laws.

Ultimately, we are interested in
homotopy coherent
algebras
\mathscr{P}\to C^{\times}
which take values in a higher category
C
(which may be incarnated in various forms, like a simplicial category or quasi-category). In case
\mathscr{P}=\mathsf{Assoc}
and
C
is the
\infty
-category of spaces such algebras are
A_{\infty}
-spaces. The investigation of coherence for algebras goes back at least to the influential work of Boardman and Vogt (
[
7
]
), while a classical treatment of coherent associativity goes back to Stasheff’s paper
[
41
]
. While homotopy coherence can be formulated in the context of topological or simplicially enriched operads via cofibrant resolutions (also known as
W
-construction due to Boardman and Vogt), we run into practical limitations again due to the amount of data involved in resolutions.

A good theory of
\infty
-operads encodes a notion of operad where coherence is recorded in the structure itself, meaning that composition of operations is well-defined up to coherent homotopy. A beautiful and successful approach in the context of categories is the quasi-categorical model of
\infty
-categories developed by Joyal (
[
28
]
) and Lurie (
[
33
]
). One of the features which makes quasi-categories practical is the fact that, being simplicial sets, many ideas from category theory, like limits and colimits, may be directly implemented semantically and the resulting notion is automatically coherent.

The latter idea is implemented by Lurie in
[
32
]
to model
\infty
-operads. An operad may be interpreted within category theory as a certain category which is equipped with a certain functor into finite pointed sets (see also
[
24
]
for a somewhat allegedly friendly introduction to the idea). Then, the latter is interpreted in the realm of
\infty
-categories using theory developed in
[
33
]
. While the resulting structure encodes coherence for operads, one could argue that the direct semantic relationship between Lurie’s model and classical operad theory is not transparent (at least when compared to the theory of quasi-categories in relation to ordinary categories).

The model we present in this paper is developed in analogy with
\infty
-category theory rather than within the theory. We develop a combinatorial framework via the theory of simplicial lists with the initial aim of creating a good environment to interpret classical operad theory in a coherent manner. Certain aspects of our approach are similar to the dendroidal approach developed by Moerdijk and his collaborators (
[
37
,
14
,
12
,
13
]
, see also the dedicated book
[
26
]
), but there are key differences which will appear as the theory is presented. Other aspects of our work are similar to models for
\infty
-operads based on Segal conditions developed by Barwick (
[
3
]
) and Chu-Haugseng (
[
11
,
10
]
).

Moreover, we believe that the same approach will eventually cover coherent versions of related structures such as properads or props (analogues of the structures in
[
20
]
, for instance). In this paper we give hints of the possibility of such an ambition to come to fruition.

Note

(Symmetries)
.

Roughly speaking, an operad has symmetries if one can permute inputs of operations to obtain new operations. Even though we believe our approach can extend to cover symmetric operads, in this paper we restrict ourselves to the non-symmetric case. While many examples of interest are symmetric, there are many which are most easily comprehensible without symmetries (like
\mathsf{Assoc}
).

Our framework

Some of the features that enable the combinatorics of simplicial sets to support
\infty
-category theory are the following:

-

Kan complexes are a model for the homotopy theory of spaces.

-

There is a fully-faithful nerve functor
N:\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{sSet}
from the category of categories.

-

There is a coherent nerve functor
N_{\Delta}:\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}\to\mathsf{sSet}
from the category of simplicial categories which, among other things, maps categories enriched in Kan complexes to
\infty
-categories.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad. As nice as simplicial sets are, we cannot define a nerve
N(\mathscr{P}):\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
such that

N(\mathscr{P})_{0}=\text{colors of }\ \mathscr{P}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ N(\mathscr{P})_{1}=\text{operations of }\ \mathscr{P}

for the evident reason that the domain assigning function
d_{1}
would not model multimorphisms. A natural line of thought is to declare that the category
\Delta
itself is deficient, as it is formed by simplex-like objects and their morphisms. The dendroidal setting developed by Moerdijk-Weiss (
[
37
]
) addresses the issue by replacing
\Delta
with a category
\Omega
whose objects are rooted trees and whose morphisms are maps of operads between the free operads generated by rooted trees. This way, one encodes all of the structure of an operad by mapping rooted trees into the operad.
We will come back to the
\Delta
-replacement strategy.

The other line of thought is to say that the blame on the failure of a nerve construction for operads lies in the notion of function. After all, why does
d_{1}
have to map elements of
N(\mathscr{P})_{1}
to elements of
N(\mathscr{P})_{0}
? This leads us to consider the notion of
listing
.

Definition 1.1

(The category of listings)
.

Let
X
be a set. We denote by
\mathscr{L}X
the set of finite sequences in
X
, i.e.

\mathscr{L}X=\displaystyle\coprod_{n\geq 0}X^{n}

A listing, denoted
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}X
, is a function
u:A\rightarrow\mathscr{L}X
.

Given composable listings
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}X
and
v:X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
, their composite is defined via concatenation, i.e. in case
a\in A
and
u(a)=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
we have

(vu)(a)=v(x_{1})*\dots*v(x_{k})

where
*
denotes the concatenation operation for sequences.

Let
\mathsf{List}
be the resulting category.

Remark 1.1
.

\mathsf{List}
is just the Kleisli category of the free monoid monad on the category of sets. Equivalently, it is the category of free monoids.
In light of this, the inclusion
\mathsf{Set}\subseteq\mathsf{List}
, which regards every function as a listing with target lists being of length
1
, has a right adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{List}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}

We do not adopt these perspectives in this paper. Rather, we think of listings as certain
multivalued functions
. For instance, here is a listing
u

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{4}}}

with
u(a_{1})=(b_{1},b_{3})
and
u(a_{2})=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{4})
. This is closer to the notion of span between sets. In fact, a listing
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}B
may be regarded as a span
A\xleftarrow{s}U\xrightarrow{t}B
in which the fibres
s^{-1}(a)
are finite and equipped with an order. Here,
U
is the set of arrows and
s,t
are functions assigning source and target. Note that
s^{-1}(a)
is allowed to be empty, which means that a listing may assign the empty sequence in the codomain to an element in the domain.

For an operad
\mathscr{P}
, instead of aiming at incarnating its nerve as a simplicial set, we will divert our ambition into creating a
list nerve

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{l}(\mathscr{P}):}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{List}}

The
d_{1}
-obstruction vanishes. Indeed, we can declare a listing
d_{1}:N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{1}{\mathrel{\to}}N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{0}
which assigns to each operation its domain sequence of colors.

For the
2
-simplices, we implement the general nerve idea

N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{2}=\text{composable pairs of operations in }\ \mathscr{P}

Let us consider the following pair of operations
(f,g)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

If we declare a
2
-simplex
\sigma
that witnesses the composite
g\circ f
, we may declare that
d_{0}\sigma=g
,
d_{2}\sigma=f
and
d_{1}\sigma=g\circ f
. However, we can compute that
d_{1}d_{1}\sigma=d_{1}(g\circ f)=(b_{1},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},b_{3})
, while on the other hand
d_{1}d_{2}\sigma=d_{1}f=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
. So, while the depicted
\sigma
would be a perfectly fine dendrex in the dendroidal nerve by virtue of being recorded as a tree, the simplicial identities seem to fail.

Again, there is a remedy. In the simplicial setting, the simplex
\sigma
should really be depicted as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{b_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{b_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}

and, taking advantage of
\mathsf{List}
, we declare that
d_{0}\sigma=g
,
d_{2}\sigma=(1_{b_{1}},f,1_{b_{3}})
and
d_{1}\sigma=g\circ f
. This way, the simplicial identities will hold and we are lead to the general pattern our attempt at a nerve construction should follow: it must be indexed by
leveled trees
.

It turns out that leveled trees of the type we are interested in are encoded by simplices of augmented simplex category category
\Delta_{+}
, which is just the category
\Delta
with the additional empty-set object
\emptyset
serving as initial object. For notation benefits, we think of the objects of
\Delta_{+}
as finite ordered sets and lie up to unique isomorphism. For example, a
2
-simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{2}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{c_{1},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},c_{2}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{c\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}

encodes the following leveled tree

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{p_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{3}}

Notice that the morphisms
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
in the formation of
\alpha
determine and are determined by the shape of the tree.

We may form the free operad
T_{\alpha}
associated to a simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
and define

N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{n}=\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\alpha},\mathscr{P})

The notation
N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
stands for
rooted simplices
of
N(\Delta_{+})
, meaning those simplices
\alpha:\Delta^{n}\to\Delta_{+}
such that
\alpha(n)\cong[0]
. Note that general
\alpha
encode ordered sequences of leveled trees
1
1

1

Such sequences of leveled trees appear in
[
25
]
as leveled forests, except that ours is a planar variant. They also appear in
[
3
]
(we comment on this in detail in Section
4.4
).
, and this gives rise to the listings in the simplicial structure. More precisely, we do have an isomorphism

N(\Delta_{+})_{n}\cong\mathscr{L}N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}

for all
[n]\in\Delta
.
Given a morphism
\theta:[k]\to[n]
and
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
, we do have a morphism of operads
T_{\theta^{*}\alpha}\to T_{\alpha}
which produces the listing

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{k}}

by precomposition.

What we have sketched above assembles into a fully faithful list nerve functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{l}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}}

where
\mathsf{sList}
is the category whose:

-

Objects are functors
X:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{List}
.

-

Morphisms are natural transformations
f:X\to Y
such that for each
[n]\in\Delta
the component
f_{n}:X_{n}\to Y_{n}
is a function.

Moreover, for an operad
\mathscr{P}
, all the face and degeneracy maps in the simplicial structure of
N^{l}(\mathscr{P})
are functional, except for the last faces
d_{n}:N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{n}{\mathrel{\to}}N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{n-1}
. The reason is simple: every simplicial operation on a rooted simplex in
N(\Delta_{+})
produces a rooted simplex, except for the last face operation which cuts the root and leaves us with a list of rooted trees. This leads us to an important class of simplicial lists, which we call
operadic
.

Definition 1.2

(Operadic simplicial lists)
.

A simplicial list
X
is said to be operadic if for all morphisms
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta
such that
\theta(k)=n
, the action map
\theta^{*}:X_{n}\to X_{k}
is a function.

Theorem 1.1

(Nerve Theorem)
.

Let
X
be a simplicial list. Then,
X
is isomorphic to
N^{l}(\mathscr{P})
for some operad
\mathscr{P}
if and only if
X
is operadic and the simplicial set
\mathscr{L}X
is (isomorphic to the nerve of) a category.

One way to interpret the above nerve theorem is to say that leveled trees and their simplicial relations are enough to fully record the structure of an operad.
Here,
\mathscr{L}X
is the simplicial set obtained as a composite
\Delta^{op}\xrightarrow{X}\mathsf{List}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{L}}\mathsf{Set}
. Its simplices are simply list of simplices of
X
and hence the listings in the simplicial structure of
X
become functions in the structure of
\mathscr{L}X
.

Definition 1.3

(Quasi-operad)
.

A quasi-operad is an operadic simplicial list
P
such that the simplicial set
\mathscr{L}P
is a quasi-category.

There is a sense in which we are justified in claiming that quasi-operads as defined above do model
\infty
-operads. This is in light of our observations and the well-established quasi-categorical model for the theory of
\infty
-categories. Depending on context, we may refer to quasi-operads as
\infty
-operads, since it is quite common in the literature (the main example being
[
33
]
) to refer to quasi-categories as
\infty
-categories.

We will provide an additional piece of evidence which indicates that our notion of
\infty
-operad actually captures some classical theory.
It is possible to construct simplicial operads
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
associated with leveled tree shapes
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
, which are well-behaved cofibrant replacements of the operads
T_{\alpha}
, and define a homotopy coherent list nerve functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}}

which assigns to a simplicial operad
\mathscr{Q}
sets of simplices

({N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q})_{n}=\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}_{n}}\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}(\mathbb{V}_{\alpha},\mathscr{Q})

for all
[n]\in\Delta
.

Theorem 1.2
.

If a simplicial operad
\mathscr{Q}
is enriched in Kan complexes,
{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q}
is a quasi-operad.

Besides the combinatorial capability of
\mathsf{sList}
to support both nerves and coherent nerves of operads, it has, at least to us, a surprising property: it is a presheaf category!

Theorem 1.3
.

There is a full subcategory
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathsf{sList}
such that
\mathsf{sList}\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\mathscr{F}^{op}}
.

We construct the subcategory
\mathscr{F}
combinatorially, but in rather abstract fashion. A purely combinatorial description of
\mathscr{F}
remains to be explored. However, when we restrict to operadic simplicial lists, we still have a presheaf structure with base the operadic objects in
\mathscr{F}
. The combinatorial structure of the latter is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.4
.

There is a full subcategory
\Upsilon\subseteq\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
of the category of operadic simplicial lists such that

\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\Upsilon^{op}}

Moreover, the category
\Upsilon
admits the following description:

-

The objects are the rooted simplices of
N(\Delta_{+})
(leveled trees).

-

Given two such simplices
\alpha\in\Upsilon^{(n)}
and
\beta\in\Upsilon^{(k)}
, where
n
and
k
indicate simplicial dimension in
N(\Delta_{+})
, a morphism
(\theta,a):[\beta]\to[\alpha]
is a pair consisting of a morphism
\theta:[k]\to[n]
and an element
a\in\alpha(\theta(k))
such that
\beta=(\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a}
. The latter indicates the rooted simplex with root
a
which appears in the sequence of rooted simplices of
\theta^{*}\alpha
.

Here is an example of a morphism in
\Upsilon

formed by first applying the simplicial operation
d_{2}
on the target, which cuts the operation
q
, and then choosing the root
b_{2}
in the remaining sequence of rooted trees.

At the end of the day, we did replace
\Delta
with a category of trees. However, we still retain the lists perspective which allows us to directly make analogy with the classical theory of simplicial sets. On the other hand, the presheaf structure allows us to perform constructions faster via Yoneda extension. In comparison to the dendroidal setting, our base category
\Upsilon
has a purely combinatorial description since the morphisms between leveled trees we consider are not all morphisms between the free operads they generate
2
2

2

This is not to say the dendroidal approach is not combinatorial. The morphisms between dendroidal trees do factor into faces, degeneracies and automorphisms. What we mean is that this combinatorial structure is formal, while the one we present is explicit.
.

Besides dendroidal sets, the referee pointed out another strong connection to already existing ideas, more precisely to the structures developed by Barwick in
[
3
]
and Chu-Haugseng in
[
11
,
10
]
. Indeed,
\Upsilon
may be regarded as a full subcategory of the category
\Delta_{\Phi}
in
[
3
, Definition 2.4]
for the case
\Phi=\Delta_{+}
. The latter category also appears as an example of an
algebraic pattern
in
[
11
, Example 3.8]
.

In both of the above bodies of work, a model for
\infty
-operads is defined via Segal conditions, mostly generalizing ideas developed by Lurie in
[
32
]
(such as operator categories or the inert-active factorization). In fact, it is easy to derive from the aforementioned references that Segal
\Upsilon
-spaces (say, as in
[
11
, Definition 2.7]
) do model
\infty
-operads.
In this paper, we work in the quasi-categorical direction as we believe this approach has advantages due to combinatorial tractability.

Organization of this paper

We recall some basic elements of the theory of operads in Section
2
, mostly with the intention of fixing notation and terminology. In Section
3
we introduce the important notion of multigraph and construct a free-forgetful adjunction with operads. Then, we take a brief detour into the free resolution this adjunction generates. Afterwards, we construct our nerve functor (already sketched to some amount of detail above) and prove our nerve theorem which motivates our model for
\infty
-operads. In Section
4
we prove our presheaf theorems. This section is the combinatorial heart of this paper, as we introduce some key techniques to deal with simplicial lists. In Section
5
we define our coherent nerve functor, building on results from the previous sections. We prove that coherent nerves of Kan-enriched operads provide examples of
\infty
-operads. Moreover, we study some properties of the rigidification functor obtained by adjunction. In Section
6
we briefly discuss a natural variant of homology for simplicial lists. The short appendices
A
and
B
are dedicated to augmented simplicial objects and free resolutions, as these structures appear quite often in this paper.

Some connections with the literature

Even though the above ideas are developed independently, many of them are not quite new. The evident connection to dendroidal theory and to the Segal-condition based notions in
[
3
]
,
[
11
]
has already been mentioned. The idea of encoding trees via multivalued functions appears in
[
29
]
. In the context of symmetric
\infty
-properads, the ideas proposed in
[
1
]
seem to be aligned with the spirit of our ideas (with a key difference being that their approach is internal to
\infty
-category theory). The idea of keeping track of levels in trees is present in
[
25
]
(in the notion of forest) where an equivalence between Lurie’s model and the dendroidal model is established, in
[
8
]
where a necklace theorem for dendroidal rigidification is presented, but also in the aforementioned
[
3
]
,
[
11
]
and
[
10
]
. Leveled graphs appear in the context of properads in
[
9
]
and are also used in
[
5
]
.

Future goals

While the scent of homotopy theory can be discerned in this paper, we do not speak of a model structure on
\mathsf{sList}
. In a sequel paper, we intend to provide a model structure on
\mathsf{sList}
which is Quillen equivalent via the coherent nerve to the model structure on
\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
(
[
39
]
). A study of the comparison to the dendroidal model, but also to Lurie’s model and Segal
\Upsilon
-spaces, should be possible via model structures.
Other model structures such as the cartesian model structure ought to be studied in the context of simplicial lists.
Another objective of ours is to develop an appropriate notion of symmetric simplicial list which models symmetric operads and
\infty
-operads.

Moreover, we expect to be able to say something about properads and
\infty
-properads via simplicial lists. For instance, we can talk about properadic simplicial list by allowing the first face maps to be listings (besides the last face maps). A priory, it is already known to us that properadic simplicial lists have a presheaf structure. The work to do is in unpacking the combinatorics and construct good nerve functors.

In fact, at least in the non-symmetric setting, an interesting object of study, which we would like to call compositional network, appears in front of us.

Definition 1.4
.

A
compositional network
is a simplicial list
X
such that
\mathscr{L}X
is (the nerve of) a category.

These objects seem to generalize both operads and properads, and their algebras might be interesting.

Another interesting direction to pursue is to check under what circumstances our constructions can be carried our when we work relative to a given monad
T
on some category
\mathscr{C}
. Is there a nerve functor which maps a
T
-multicategory (in the sense of Leinster
[
30
, Definition 4.2.2]
) to a simplicial object in the Kleisli category
\mathscr{C}_{T}
? In fact, the special case of the monad
fc
on
\mathsf{Cat}
induced by the free -forgetful adjunction with graphs, leads to the notion of virtual double category. A homotopy coherent version has been worked out, in the style of Lurie, in
[
18
]
in order to study enrichment.

While we briefly discuss a variant of homology for simplicial lists, other variants might be possible and more sophisticated results and applications are to be searched for.

Some conventions

In general, we name categories after the name of their objects. For example,
\mathsf{Set}
is the category of sets,
\mathsf{Op}
is the category of operads,
\mathsf{Ab}
is the category of abelian groups etc. One exception is the category
\mathsf{List}
which is named after its morphisms.

When it comes to simplicial objects we follow fairly standard notation.
\Delta
is the simplex category, or equivalently the category of finite ordinals, whose objects we denote
[n]=\{0,\dots,n\}
. A simplicial set if a functor
X:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
. We denote by
X_{n}
the image of
[n]
under
X
. For a general morphism
\theta:[n]\to[m]
we write
\theta^{*}:X_{m}\to X_{n}
for the image of
\theta
under the functor
X
, while we denote by
d_{i}
and
s_{i}
the face and degeneracy functions.

We denote by
\Delta_{+}
the augmented simplex category, obtained by adjoining an initial object
\emptyset
to
\Delta
. For notational benefits, we regard any finite ordered set as an object in
\Delta_{+}
. We are justified in this abuse since for any finite ordered set
A
there is a unique
[n]\in\Delta_{+}
equipped with a unique isomorphism
A\cong[n]
.

As for size issues, while we do not comment much on them in this paper, there is nothing problematic going on. For instance, one can apply the technology of Grothendieck universes. We refer the reader to
[
40
]
for more details on the subject.

2
Some elements of the theory of operads

We briefly recall some elements of the theory of operads which are relevant to us, mainly for the purpose of fixing notation and language. We are particularly interested in the relationship between operads and categories.

Definition 2.1

(Colored operad)
.

A (colored, non-symmetric) operad
\mathscr{P}
consists of a collection of colors and operations of the form
f:{\underline{a}}\to b
which admit as domain sequences of colors
{\underline{a}}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
. These collections are denoted by
\text{Col}(\mathscr{P})
and
\text{Op}(\mathscr{P})
respectively, while
\mathscr{P}({\underline{a}},b)
is the set of operations with specified source and target. Typically, an operation
f:{\underline{a}}\to b
is depicted as a corolla

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}

An operation
g:{\underline{b}}\to c
may be composed with a sequence of operations
(f_{i}:{\underline{a}}^{(i)}\to b_{i})_{i}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}^{(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n_{1}}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}

to produce an operation
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{k}):{\underline{a}}^{(1)}*\dots*{\underline{a}}^{(k)}\to c
whose domain the concatenation of the sequences on the top of the tree. For each color
a\in\mathscr{P}
, there is an identity operation
1_{a}:a\to a
. The composition operation is associative and unital.

Remark 2.1
.

In this paper, the term operad always means
colored non-symmetric operad
. The literature varies on this point. Sometimes symmetries are assumed, sometimes a single color is postulated, and sometimes the colors and symmetries are postulated in a definition of operad.

Remark 2.2
.

The general composition scheme postulated in the definition also entails composition along a single input, by inserting identities at the other inputs. For example, in case we have
f:a\to b_{1}
and
g:(b_{1},b_{2})\to c
we may form
g\circ f=g\circ(f,1_{b_{2}})

It is possible to rewrite the definition of operad in terms of single input composites.

Definition 2.2

(Morphisms a.k.a. algebras)
.

A morphism of operads
\phi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is a structure preserving map, i.e. it consists of functions on colors and operations which respect source, target, identities and composition. Let
\mathsf{Op}
be the category of operads under this notion of morphism.
Depending on context, morphisms
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
are also referred to as
\mathscr{P}
-algebras in
\mathscr{Q}
.

Note that the definition of operad allows the empty sequence of colors
\emptyset
in the domain. Operations of the form
\emptyset\to b
are called
nullary
, while operations of the form
a\to b
with domain a singleton sequence are called
unary
. Hence, every operad
\mathscr{P}
has incorporated in it a category
[\mathscr{P}]_{1}
whose objects are the colors of
\mathscr{P}
and morphisms are the unary operations of
\mathscr{P}
. In fact, we have a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[-\right]_{1}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}}

which is right adjoint to the inclusion functor
\mathsf{Cat}\subseteq\mathsf{Op}
which regards every category as an operad with unary operations only. This is the first interesting relationship between operads and categories.

Definition 2.3

(Equivalence of operads)
.

A morphism of operads
\phi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is said to be an equivalence if it induces bijections on operation sets and it is essentially surjective. The latter means that
[\phi]_{1}
is essentially surjective.

Remark 2.3

(Enriched operads)
.

Operads accept enrichment over any monoidal category
\mathscr{V}
. This is to say that there is a
\mathscr{V}
-object of operations
\mathscr{P}({\underline{a}},b)
for all sequences of colors
{\underline{a}}
and color
b
such that the relevant diagrams in defining an operad commute in
\mathscr{V}
. In this paper we are interested in
\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
, the category of operads enriched in simplicial sets. Enrichment for operads is discussed in far greater generality in
[
30
, Section 6.8]
.

Example 2.1

(Monoidal categories)
.

As mentioned in the introduction, every monoidal category
(\mathscr{V},\otimes,1_{\mathscr{V}})
gives rise to a colored operad
\mathscr{V}^{\otimes}
whose colors are the objects of
\mathscr{V}
and operations
\underline{A}\to B
are morphisms of the form
A_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes A_{k}\to B
.

\hbox to109.81pt{\vbox to66.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 54.90268pt\lower-33.42368pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-54.90268pt}{-29.76395pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -75.97 -41.18)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{

Morphisms
1_{\mathscr{V}}\to A
from the unit of the monoidal structure are regarded as nullary operations, while composition of operations is provided by composition in
\mathscr{V}
(and the fact that
\otimes
is functorial).
It is easy to see that a monoidal functor gives rise to a morphism of operads, so that the construction
(\mathscr{V},\otimes,1_{\mathscr{V}})\mapsto\mathscr{V}^{\otimes}
defines a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\otimes}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{MonCat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}

from the category of monoidal categories to operads.

Example 2.2

(The monoidal envelope)
.

Each operad
\mathscr{P}
gives rise to a monoidal category, called the monoidal envelope of
\mathscr{P}
, which we denote
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
(recall that for a set
X
,
\mathscr{L}X
denotes the set of finite sequences in
X
). This category can be described as follows:

-

Its objects are sequences of colors of
\mathscr{P}
, i.e.
\text{Ob}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})=\mathscr{L}\text{Col}(\mathscr{P})
.

-

Its morphisms are sequences of operations in
\mathscr{P}
, i.e.
\text{Mor}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})=\mathscr{L}\text{Op}(\mathscr{P})
. For instance,

\hbox to80.45pt{\vbox to70.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.04411pt\lower-35.3195pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-75.66266pt}{21.37733pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -104.69 29.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

-

Source, target and identities are assigned in the evident manner, while composition is provided by composing operations in
\mathscr{P}
.

-

The monoidal structure is provided by concatenation of sequences.

A non-internal way to describe
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
is to say that, for two sequences of colors
{\underline{a}}:A\to\text{Col}(\mathscr{P})
and
{\underline{b}}:B\to\text{Col}(\mathscr{P})
indexed by ordered sets
A
and
B
, we have

\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}({\underline{a}},{\underline{b}})=\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha:A\to B}\left(\bigtimes_{b\in B}\mathscr{P}({\underline{a}}_{b},b)\right)

where
\alpha:A\to B
signifies a morphism in
\Delta_{+}
and
{\underline{a}}_{b}
indicates the restriction of
{\underline{a}}
to
\alpha^{-1}(b)
. We obtain a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{MonCat}}

although many times we disregard the monoidal structure in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
. Notice that the
second description of
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
can be extended to cover the enriched case (if the base
monoidal category has finite coproducts which distribute over the monoidal product, which is the case for complete and cocomplete cartesian monoidal categories).

This construction is discussed in
[
30
, Section 2.3]
, while the term monoidal envelope was introduced by Lurie in
[
32
, Section 2.2.4]
in the context of
\infty
-operads.

Example 2.3

(The associative operad)
.

We already discussed
\mathsf{Assoc}
, the terminal operad which records monoid laws. Note that we have an isomorphism
\mathscr{L}\mathsf{Assoc}\cong\Delta_{+}
.

Remark 2.4

(Simplicial operads)
.

Just as in the case of simplicial categories, we may regard a simplicial operad
\mathscr{Q}
as a simplicial object
\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet}:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{Op}
which is
discrete
. For
[n]\in\Delta
, the operad
\mathscr{Q}_{n}
has as colors those of
\mathscr{Q}
and
n
-simplices in the operation spaces of
\mathscr{Q}
as operations. We say
\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet}
is discrete to indicate the fact that simplicial operations are identity-on-colors morphisms of operads.

In this vein, we may identify
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
with the discrete simplicial category
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet}
. In other words, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Op}^{\Delta^{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Cat}^{\Delta^{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

where the functor on the bottom is obtained by applying
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{Op}\to\mathsf{Cat}
dimension-wise.
We will freely use these identifications in this paper.

Lemma 2.1
.

The functor
\mathscr{L}
is left adjoint to
(-)^{\otimes}
.

Proof.

This is easy to verify.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

The adjunction
(\mathscr{L},(-)^{\otimes})
is in fact a free-forgetful one. It is apparent how
\mathscr{L}
is free, but perhaps it is not clear how
(-)^{\otimes}
is forgetful. See
[
30
, Theorem 3.3.4]
for a characterisation of monoidal categories as operads with some extra conditions.

Example 2.4

(The
\mathsf{Hom}
operad)
.

Let
S
be a fixed set. There is an operad
\mathsf{Hom}_{S}
with set of colors
S\times S
and, for each sequence of elements
a_{1},\dots a_{n}\in S
, a unique operation
((a_{1},a_{2}),\dots,(a_{n-1},a_{n}))\to(a_{1},a_{n})
, and for each element
a\in S
a unique nullary operation
\emptyset\to(a,a)
. Composition is defined in the evident manner. An algebra
\mathsf{Hom}_{S}\to\mathsf{Set}^{\times}
is precisely a category with set of objects
S
, while algebras values in a monoidal category
\mathscr{V}
are
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories with set of objects
S
.

This operad appears as Example 2.4 in
[
37
]
for instance. Our choice of notation is to indicate that, in creating an algebra, we equip the set
S
with hom-objects in a monoidal category of choice.

On the homotopical front, we are interested in the behaviour of the functor
\mathscr{L}
. The connected components functor
\pi_{0}:\mathsf{sSet}\to\mathsf{Set}
preserves products and hence induces a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}

which takes connected components of operation spaces in a simplicial operad.

Lemma 2.2
.

The following square commutes (up to natural isomorphism)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{Q}
be a simplicial operad. The categories
\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})
and
\mathscr{L}(\pi_{0}\mathscr{Q})
both have
\mathscr{L}\text{Col}(\mathscr{Q})
as set of objects.
Let
{\underline{a}}:A\to\text{Col}(\mathscr{Q})
and
{\underline{b}}:B\to\text{Col}(\mathscr{Q})
be two sequences of colors. The mapping space in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
is given by

(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})({\underline{a}},{\underline{b}})=\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha:A\to B}(\bigtimes_{b\in B}\mathscr{Q}({\underline{a}}_{b},b))

Since
\pi_{0}
preserves coproducts (besides products), we have a natural isomorphism
\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})({\underline{a}},{\underline{b}})\cong\mathscr{L}(\pi_{0}\mathscr{Q}({\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}))
.

∎

The canonical model structure on
\mathsf{Cat}
, which has as weak equivalences the equivalences of categories and as fibrations isofibrations
3
3

3

Recall that a functor
F:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
between categories is an isofibration in case any isomorphism in
\mathscr{B}
of the form
F(a)\xrightarrow{\cong}b
lifts to an isomorphism
a\xrightarrow{\cong}b^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{A}
.
, extends to operads in straightforward fashion.

Theorem 2.1

(Canonical model structure)
.

There is a model structure on
\mathsf{Op}
in which a morphism
\phi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is declared to be:

-

A weak equivalence if it is an equivalence of operads.

-

A fibration if the underlying functor
[\phi]_{1}:[\mathscr{P}]_{1}\to[\mathscr{Q}]_{1}
is an isofibration.

Proof.

The statement and proof appears as Theorem 1.6.2 in
[
42
]
, but also in
[
39
, Theorem 2]
. The proofs are written for the symmetric case, but hold without symmetries as well.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

The functor
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{Op}\to\mathsf{Cat}
preserves weak equivalences and fibrations (and hence trivial fibrations).

Proof.

Let
\phi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
be a morphism of operads. It is clear that if
\phi
is an equivalence of operads, then
\mathscr{L}\phi
is an equivalence of categories. Assume
\phi
is a fibration. Any isomorphism
\phi({\underline{a}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underline{b}}
in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
is determined by a sequence of isomorphisms
(\phi(a_{i})\xrightarrow{\cong}b_{i})_{i}
in
[\mathscr{Q}]_{1}
. Since
[\phi]_{1}
is an isofibration, we can lift the latter sequence term by term to an isomorphism
{\underline{a}}\to{\underline{b}}^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
.
∎

In similar fashion, the Bergner model structure on simplicial categories (
[
6
]
) extends to simplicial operads.

Theorem 2.2

(Model structure on simplicial operads)
.

The category
\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
of simplicial operads has model structure in which a morphism
\phi:\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{Q}
is said to be a weak equivalence if

(W1)

For all sequences of colors
{\underline{x}}
and color
y
in
\mathscr{K}
, the morphism of simplicial sets
\phi_{{\underline{x}},y}:\mathscr{K}({\underline{x}},y)\to\mathscr{Q}(\phi{\underline{x}},\phi y)
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets.

(W2)

The morphism of categories
\pi_{0}[\phi]_{1}:\pi_{0}[\mathscr{K}]_{1}\to\pi_{0}[\mathscr{Q}]_{1}
is an equivalence.

and a fibration if

(F1)

For all sequences of colors
{\underline{x}}
and color
y
in
\mathscr{K}
, the morphism of simplicial sets
\phi_{{\underline{x}},y}:\mathscr{K}({\underline{x}},y)\to\mathscr{Q}(\phi{\underline{x}},\phi y)
is a fibration of simplicial sets.

(F2)

The morphism of categories
\pi_{0}[\phi]_{1}:\pi_{0}[\mathscr{K}]_{1}\to\pi_{0}[\mathscr{Q}]_{1}
is an isofibration.

Proof.

This is stated and proved for symmetric operads in
[
39
, Theorem 6]
, but holds without symmetries as well.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

The functor
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}\to\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}
preserves weak equivalences and fibrations (and hence, trivial fibrations).

Proof.

Suppose
\phi:\mathscr{K}\to\mathscr{Q}
is a weak equivalence of simplicial operads. Let
{\underline{a}}:A\to\text{Col}(\mathscr{K})
and
{\underline{b}}:B\to\text{Col}(\mathscr{K})
be two objects in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{K}
. The morphism on mapping spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{L}\phi)_{{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha:A\to B}\left(\bigtimes_{b\in B}\mathscr{K}({\underline{a}}_{b},b)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha:A\to B}\left(\bigtimes_{b\in B}\mathscr{Q}(\phi{\underline{a}}_{b},\phi b)\right)}

is componentwise a weak equivalence of simplicial sets, and hence it is a weak equivalence itself. The functor
\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}\phi):\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{K})\to\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})
is also an equivalence of categories because, by the previous lemma we have
\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}\phi)\cong\mathscr{L}(\pi_{0}\phi)
and, since
\pi_{0}\phi
is an equivalence of operads and
\mathscr{L}
preserves equivalences,
\mathscr{L}(\pi_{0}\phi)
is an equivalence.

In case
\phi
is a fibration, it is clear by the above expression of the map induced by
\mathscr{L}\phi
on mapping spaces that the latter is a fibration of simplicial sets (fibrations of simplicial sets are closed under products and disjoint unions). The fact that
\pi_{0}(\mathscr{L}\phi)
is an isofibration, as in the previous argument, follows from the fact that
\mathscr{L}
commutes with
\pi_{0}
and that on the level of categories
\mathscr{L}
preserves isofibrations.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

We have used the letter
\mathscr{L}
in denoting both the functor
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{List}\to\mathsf{Set}
, which maps a set
X
to the set of lists
\mathscr{L}X
, and the monoidal envelope
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{Op}\to\mathsf{Cat}
. One reason for this slight abuse is the fact that, for an operad
\mathscr{P}
, the category
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
is formed by taking lists of colors and operations in
\mathscr{P}
. Further justification will be provided when we construct nerves of operads in the next section.

An interesting fact to point out is that the functor
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{List}\to\mathsf{Set}
is right adjoint to the inclusion
\mathsf{Set}\subseteq\mathsf{List}
. On the other hand, the functor
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{Op}\to\mathsf{Cat}
is left adjoint when we modify the target to be monoidal categories. But, we can also exhibit
\mathscr{L}
as a right adjoint by modifying the notion of morphism between operads.

More precisely, we may form a category
\widetilde{\mathsf{Op}}
in which the objects are operads and morphisms between two operads
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are monoidal functors
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
, which we regard as
listings
between operads. In other words,
\widetilde{\mathsf{Op}}
is the category of free monoidal categories (in the sense that they are generated by operads). Equivalently,
\widetilde{\mathsf{Op}}
is the Kleisli category associated the free monoidal category monad on operads. Notice that if
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are sets (meaning that they consist only of colors) a listing of operads is the same as a listing of sets.

This way, it is easy to see that the inclusion
\mathsf{Cat}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathsf{Op}}
, which regards every category as an operad with unary operations only, has as right adjoint the functor
\mathscr{L}:\widetilde{\mathsf{Op}}\to\mathsf{Cat}
which maps
\mathscr{P}
to
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
and each morphism
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
to itself. Indeed, for a category
\mathscr{A}
and an operad
\mathscr{P}
we have bijections

\mathsf{Cat}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\cong\mathsf{MonCat}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\cong\widetilde{\mathsf{Op}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{P})

3
First constructions

3.1
Simplicial lists

The category
\mathsf{List}
has sets as objects and its morphisms
A{\mathrel{\to}}X
are functions
A\to\mathscr{L}X
, while composition happens by concatenation (Definition
1.1
). We are interested in the following categories:

-

\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}=\mathsf{List}^{\Delta^{op}}
, the category of simplicial objects in
\mathsf{List}
.

-

\mathsf{sList}
, the wide subcategory of
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
with morphisms required to have functions as components.

-

\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}}
and
\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
, the full subcategories of
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
and
\mathsf{sList}
spanned by operadic simplicial lists (Definition
1.2
). The latter are simplicial objects
X:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{List}
such that all face and degeneracy operations are functions, except possibly for the last face maps
d_{n}:X_{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X_{n-1}
,
n\geq 1
, which are allowed to be listings.

For multiple reasons, we concentrate on the categories
\mathsf{sList}
and
\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
. Operadic simplicial lists are important because they “look” like operads. For instance, if
X\in\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
, a
1
-simplex
f\in X_{1}
has a face
d_{0}f
which is a single element of
X_{0}
, while the face
d_{1}f
may be a sequence in
\mathscr{L}X_{0}
, thus allowing us to imagine
f
in the form of a corolla

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}

We will prove in Section
4.4
that higher simplices in operadic simplicial lists look like leveled trees.

Then, a key reason to want morphisms between simplicial lists to be functional is the fact that morphisms of operads preserve the number of inputs in operations. For instance, let
\phi:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
and let
f\in X_{1}
be a
1
-simplex as above. The simplicial identities dictate that
d_{1}\phi(f)=\phi(d_{1}f)
. In other words, we must have that
d_{1}\phi(f)=(\phi a_{1},\dots,\phi a_{k})
, so that
\phi(f)
is depicted as a corolla

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(a_{k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(a_{1})}

with
k
input leaves.

Another important reason to require morphisms to be functional has to do with
colimits
. The category
\mathsf{List}
is not cocomplete and hence, neither is
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
. But, since the existence of colimits is not determined by the objects of a category, but by its morphisms, the problem vanishes when working in
\mathsf{sList}
. We prove that
\mathsf{sList}
is cocomplete in Section
4
, but it is not difficult to believe this result given that morphisms are component-wise functions of sets.

Note
.

There is also an explanatory account from the double categorical perspective.
Listings and functions are two notions of morphisms between sets. We may form a Kleisli double category with listings as horizontal morphisms, functions as vertical morphisms and 2-cells being commutative squares in
\mathsf{List}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

This way, we may interpret
\mathsf{sList}
as the category of functors from
\Delta^{op}
into the horizontal part of this double category. Then, our notion of morphism coincides with the notion of
vertical transformation
(see Definition 3 in
[
21
]
), which appears in the study of double colimits.

The functor
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{List}\to\mathsf{Set}
, which is right adjoint of the inclusion
\mathsf{Set}\subseteq\mathsf{List}
, gives us a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sSet}}

by post-composition. For all
X\in\mathsf{sList}
, the simplicial set
\mathscr{L}X
has as
n
-simplices lists of
n
-simplices in
X
. In particular,
\mathscr{L}X
is a monoid object in
\mathsf{sSet}
. So, this is just like the monoidal envelope functor for operads . In fact, we will see in
3.4
that it extends the monoidal envelope functor.

\mathscr{L}
is no longer right adjoint though, but it is so if we extend the domain from
\mathsf{sList}
to
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
. This is where the latter category becomes useful. For instance, given a simplicial list
X
, we have the following bijections

\mathscr{L}X_{n}\cong\mathsf{sSet}(\Delta^{n},\mathscr{L}X)\cong\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}(\Delta^{n},X)

This tells us that a simplex
x\in X_{n}
is represented by a listing
(x):\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X
when we regard
x
as a singleton sequence in
\mathscr{L}X_{n}
. This is an important fact, since we don’t know much else about what simplices in a general simplicial list look like. We illustrate the latter point with an example.

Example 3.1

(A wild simplicial list)
.

We may define a (truncated) simplicial list
X:\Delta^{op}_{\leq 1}\to\mathsf{List}
with
X_{0}=\{a\}
and
X_{1}=\{s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\}
. Let the simplicial operations be as follows:

d_{0}s^{\prime}=\emptyset\ ,\ d_{1}s^{\prime}=a\ ,\ d_{0}s^{\prime\prime}=a\ ,\ d_{1}s^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset\ ,\ s_{0}a=(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})

The action of the degeneracy map
s_{0}
on
a
may be depicted operadically as follows:

\hbox to18.61pt{\vbox to32.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.30553pt\lower-16.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-9.30553pt}{-12.65974pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -12.88 -17.52)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \ \mapsto\ \ \hbox to66.51pt{\vbox to65.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 33.25688pt\lower-32.73814pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
