}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{21.40483pt}{-17.9256pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 29.62 -24.8)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The simplicial identities are visibly satisfied. The “wild” part has to do with the fact that both
s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
are non-degenerate, while the list
(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime})
is.

We can make the example more wild by letting
X_{1}=\{s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime},x\}
with
s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
as above and say that
d_{0}x=d_{1}x=\emptyset
while
s_{0}a=(s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime},x)
. This doesn’t violate the simplicial identities. The situation can get more complex if we allow higher simplices. We believe this flexibility of general simplicial lists can help model interesting phenomena.

Besides the monoidal envelope, we also have an underlying simplicial set functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[-\right]_{1}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sSet}}

which assigns to a simplicial list
X
the simplicial set whose
n
-simplices are given by
[X]_{1}([n])=\mathsf{sList}(\Delta^{n},X)
.
[-]_{1}
is the right adjoint of the inclusion functor
\mathsf{sSet}\subseteq\mathsf{sList}
.

3.2
Multigraphs and free operads

In ordinary category theory, an important tool in describing categories of interest is the notion of free category generated by a graph (see Appendix
B
). We develop an analog in the context of operads, that is, free operads generated from
multigraphs
.

Definition 3.1

(Multigraph)
.

Let
\Delta^{op}_{\leq 1}
be the full subcategory of
\Delta
spanned by the objects
[0]
and
[1]
.
A (reflexive, directed) multigraph is a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{op}_{\leq 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{List}}

which is operadic when regarded as a simplicial list.

A morphism of multigraphs is a natural transformation in which the components are functions. We denote by
\mathsf{MGraph}
the resulting category.

A multigraph
M
consists of two sets
\mathscr{M}_{0}
and
\mathscr{M}_{1}
related by operations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{M}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{M}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}

which satisfy the simplicial identities. The condition of being operadic specifies that
s_{0}
and
d_{0}
are functions, while
d_{1}
is allowed to be a listing. Therefore, an edge of
\mathscr{M}
has a unique target in
\mathscr{M}_{0}
, but possibly a source consisting whole sequence in
\mathscr{M}_{0}
.
Our notion of multigraph is a reflexive variant of Leinster’s
T
-graphs (
[
30
, Definition 4.2.4]
), when taking
T
to be the free monoid monad on sets (see also the remark at the end of the section).
Although more general, multigraphs can be used as models for trees.

Example 3.2

(Trees as multigraphs)
.

Consider the following tree:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

We may define a multigraph
\mathscr{M}
with
\mathscr{M}_{0}=\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},c\}
and
\mathscr{M}_{1}=\{f,g\}\amalg\{1_{x}\ :\ x\in\mathscr{M}_{0}\}
, with
d_{0}
,
d_{1}
and
s_{0}
defined in the evident manner.
We see that
\mathscr{M}
encodes all the information about the tree we depicted.

There is a forgetful functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{MGraph}}

For an operad
\mathscr{P}
, the multigraph
U\mathscr{P}
is given by

U\mathscr{P}_{0}=\text{Col}(\mathscr{P})\ \ \ ,\ \ \ U\mathscr{P}_{1}=\text{Op}(\mathscr{P})

where the simplicial operations
d_{0}
,
d_{1}
and
s_{0}
assign target, source and identity.

We discuss and construct the left adjoint free operad functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{MGraph}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}

In some sense, given a multigraph
\mathscr{M}
, it is clear how to construct
F\mathscr{M}
: compose everything you can, let the degeneracies serve as identities and make sure composition is associative. If we accept that this construction yields a left adjoint functor
F
, most of the results which rely on
F
may be proved without reference to an explicit construction.
However, for the sake of both rigor and explanatory power, we introduce some combinatorial formalism.

Definition 3.2

(Operation matrix)
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a multigraph. An operation vector in
\mathscr{M}

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\end{bmatrix}

is a composable sequence of 1-simplices in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}_{1}
such that
f_{n}
is a singleton sequence. We denote the set of such operation vectors
\mathsf{Vect}(\mathscr{M})
.

An operation matrix is a matrix

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}^{(1)}&\dots&f_{n}^{(1)}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
f_{1}^{(k)}&\dots&f_{n}^{(k)}\end{bmatrix}

where each row is an operation vector. We denote the set of such operation matrices by
\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})
.

Remark 3.1

(Empty operations)
.

In an operation vector, we allow for a symbol
1_{\emptyset}
, which we interpret as a dummy operation with no inputs and outputs. This originates from the fact that there is an empty list
\emptyset\in\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}_{0}
.
1_{\emptyset}
represents the degeneracy of the empty list in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}_{1}
.

For instance, in case
f:\emptyset\to a
is a 1-simplex in
\mathscr{M}
with output
a
and empty set of inputs, the vector

\begin{bmatrix}1_{\emptyset}&1_{\emptyset}&f\end{bmatrix}

is a valid element in
\mathsf{Vect}(\mathscr{M})
.

Notation 3.1

(Degenerate operations)
.

Whenever we write the entry “
1
” in an operation matrix we mean that the
1
-simplex in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}
this entry represents is degenerate. For instance, if
f:(a_{1},a_{2})\to b
is an operation in
\mathscr{M}_{1}
, then in the operation vector

\begin{bmatrix}1&f\end{bmatrix}

we read the entry
1
to mean the
1
-simplex
\binom{s_{0}a_{1}}{s_{0}a_{2}}
in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}_{1}
.

Note
.

Informally, we can say that an operation vector in
\mathscr{M}
is a leveled tree of operations in
\mathscr{M}
. For example, a leveled tree in
\mathscr{M}
depicted as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}

is recorded via the operation vector

\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}

In this vein, operation matrices record sequences of leveled trees (or, leveled forest). A formal formulation of this idea is given in the next Section
3.4
, Remark
3.4
.

Given composable operation matrices
M
and
N
(meaning that the output sequence of
M
coincides with the input sequence of
N
), we may form their
concatenation
, which we denote
M\odot N
, in the evident manner. For instance,

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\odot\begin{bmatrix}g\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}

We may write concatenation as a function

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})\times_{\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}_{0}}\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})}

In spirit, the free operad
F\mathscr{M}
has as colors the set
\mathscr{M}_{0}
and as operations the set
\mathsf{Vect}(\mathscr{M})
, with composition provided by concatenation. However, we must impose identifications in order to make sure unitality laws are satisfied.
Hence, we are interested in the following equivalence relations relations on
\mathsf{Vect}(\mathscr{M})
and
\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})
:

(U)

(Unitality). Two operation vectors are equivalent if one can be obtained by the other by adding or inserting
1
’s in the vector formation, i.e. this is the equivalence relation generated by

\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\end{bmatrix}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&\dots&1&\dots&f_{n}\end{bmatrix}

(RU)

(Row-wise unitality). Two operation matrices are equivalent if they are equivalent row-wise.

(OU)

(Operadic unitality). We say that two operation matrices
M
and
N
are equivalent in case we can write
M=M_{1}\odot K\odot M_{2}
and
N=M_{1}\odot K^{\prime}\odot M_{2}
and we have that
K
is equivalent to
K^{\prime}
under the relation (RU).

We denote by
\mathsf{Vect}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})
and
\mathsf{Mat}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})
the quotients of
\mathsf{Vect}(\mathscr{M})
and
\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})
with respect to the operadic unitality relation (OU) respectively. Notice that the relation (OU) is stronger that (RU).

Lemma 3.1
.

The concatenation operation on representatives induces a well-defined operation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})\times_{\mathscr{L}\mathscr{M}_{0}}\mathsf{Mat}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})}

Proof.

This is clear by construction.
∎

For a multigraph
\mathscr{M}
, each matrix in
\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})
splits into its sequence of row vectors. We may write this as a function

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{split}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\mathscr{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathsf{Vect}(\mathscr{M})}

However, given a sequence of operation vectors, we cannot obtain an operation matrix from them since the chosen list of rows may contain elements of various lengths. This obstruction vanishes when we apply the relations (U) and (RU), and hence also (OU).

Lemma 3.2

(The split-pack bijection)
.

The function

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Split}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathsf{Vect}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})}

which splits a matrix into its list of rows is well-defined and a bijection.

Proof.

The function
\mathsf{Split}
is well defined since the relation (RU) is defined row by row. We may define the inverse

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Pack}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathsf{Vect}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})}

to be given by choosing representatives of the same length in the sequence in the domain (this is always possible, by adding or removing
1
’s), packing them into a matrix and mapping the matrix into its class in
\mathsf{Mat}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})
.
\mathsf{Split}
and
\mathsf{Pack}
are clearly inverses of each other.
∎

Construction 3.1

(Free operad)
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a multigraph. We define the free operad
F\mathscr{M}
as follows:

-

On colors, let
\text{Col}(F\mathscr{M})=\mathscr{M}_{0}
.

-

On operations, let
\text{Op}(F\mathscr{M})=\mathsf{Vect}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})
.

-

For a color
a
, let the identity operation be defined by the class of the operation vector consisting of the degenerate
1
-simplex at
a
, i.e.
1_{a}=[s_{0}a]
.

-

Composition provided by concatenation.

Notice that in defining composition, we are implicitly using the split-pack bijection of the previous lemma, in the following sense. Given an operation vector
G\in\mathsf{Vect}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})
and a sequence of vectors
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}\in\mathsf{Vect}_{OU}(\mathscr{M})
which are composable in the operadic sense, we define the composite as

G\circ(G_{1},\dots,G_{k})=\mathsf{Pack}(G_{1},\dots,G_{k})\odot G

It is easy to see that this composition operation is unital and associative.

Example 3.3
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be the multigraph which encodes the following tree

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}

The free operadic composites
g\circ(f_{1},f_{2})
,
g\circ f_{1}
and
g\circ f_{2}
are encoded in
F\mathscr{M}
by the following operation vectors

\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{1_{b_{2}}}&g\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1_{b_{1}}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}

Notice the importance of imposing (OU) on operation vectors. Consider the vector

\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\end{bmatrix}

There is no entry
1
in this vector. Nonetheless, we do have

\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1&f_{1}\\
f_{2}&1\end{bmatrix}\odot\begin{bmatrix}g\end{bmatrix}

and since

\begin{bmatrix}1&f_{1}\\
f_{2}&1\end{bmatrix}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix}

we obtain

\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\end{bmatrix}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}

as operation vectors. Hence, as expected, they represent the same operation in
F\mathscr{M}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

The free operad construction
\mathscr{M}\mapsto F\mathscr{M}
is functorial. Moreover, it defines a left adjoint to the forgetful functor
U:\mathsf{Op}\to\mathsf{MGraph}
.

Proof.

Functoriality of
F
is clear. Also, we do have an evident inclusion
\eta:\mathscr{M}\to UF(\mathscr{M})
which is identity on
0
-simplices and maps every
1
-simplex
f\in\mathscr{M}_{1}
into the operation vector
[f]\in\mathsf{Vect}_{OU}(UF(\mathscr{M}))
.

We also have a natural morphism of operads
\epsilon_{\mathscr{P}}:FU(\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{P}
which is identity on colors and defined on operations by composition, i.e.

\epsilon\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}&\dots&f_{n}\end{bmatrix}=f_{n}\circ\dots\circ f_{1}

It is easy to verify that the triangle identities are satisfied.
∎

Remark 3.2

(Non-reflexive variant)
.

The notion of multigraph we employed is a special case of the notion of
T
-graph defined by Leinster (
[
30
, Definition 4.2.4]
) when taking
T
to be the free monoid monad on the category of sets, except that it is reflexive (meaning we include degeneracies). We find the reflexive version a more natural recipient for the forgetful functor from operads.

The two notions are functionally the same: we can turn any non-reflexive multigraph into a reflexive one by adding degeneracies and then apply the free operad construction as above. A saddle issue to point out is the fact that defining the free operad construction without degeneracies in the multigraph would require us to formulate a notion of tree of operations, as operation vectors would not suffice. Following the general pattern described in the introduction, we want to work with leveled trees, and hence the utility of reflexive multigraphs.

3.3
Interlude : free resolutions of operads

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad and
\mathscr{Q}
be a simplicial
4
4

4

\mathscr{Q}
may also be assumed to be enriched in topological spaces, but our default setting is enrichment over simplicial sets.
operad. There are various ways in which one can codify the notion of a
\mathscr{P}
-indexed homotopy coherent algebra in
\mathscr{Q}
, like using the classical
W
-construction of Boardman and Vogt (
[
7
]
,
[
26
, Section 1.7]
), a simplicial version of which is constructed in
[
26
, Section 2.7.7]
in terms of trees. The idea is the same: replace
\mathscr{P}
with a thick variant
W\mathscr{P}
and study morphisms
W\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
.

Our formalism offers a direct path towards thickening. The free-forgetful adjunction
F:\mathsf{MGraph}\leftrightarrows\mathsf{Op}:U
induces a free resolution functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU_{*}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}

and thus, for each operad
\mathscr{P}
, we obtain a thickened version
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
.
This way, in analogy with traditional simplicial theory, we could define a
homotopy coherent algebra
of an operad
\mathscr{P}
valued in a simplicial operad
\mathscr{Q}
to be a morphism of simplicial operads
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{Q}
.

Following intuition from the free resolution for categories (Appendix
B
) and our constructions in the previous section, we could sketch a description
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
as follows:

-

The colors of
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
are those of
\mathscr{P}
.

-

The
0
-operations are represented by operation vectors in
\mathscr{P}

\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&\dots&\cdot\end{bmatrix}

-

The
1
-operations are represented by operation vectors in
FU\mathscr{P}
. These will be vectors whose entries are operation matrices in
\mathscr{P}
, for instance

\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&\cdot\\
\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot\cdot\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

-

The face map
d_{0}
concatenates the matrices inside the operation vector. This can be thought of as removing the inner brackets of the matrices. For instance,

d_{0}\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&\cdot\\
\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot\cdot\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\binom{\cdot}{\cdot}&\binom{\cdot}{\cdot}&\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}

The face map
d_{1}
composes the entries inside each (row of each) matrix and then removes brackets

d_{1}\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&\cdot\\
\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot\cdot\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\binom{\cdot\circ\cdot}{\cdot\circ\cdot}&\cdot&\cdot\end{bmatrix}

The degeneracy
s_{0}
acts by applying inner brackets

s_{0}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&\dots&\cdot\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\cdot&\dots&\cdot\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

-

The
2
-operations will be represented by operation vectors whose entries are matrices whose entries are matrices. And so on in higher dimension. Faces are obtained by removing brackets and concatenating, except for the face
d_{0}
which composes entries. Degeneracies are formed by adding a layer of brackets.

While the above sketch provides some intuition, there are relations in each layer of the resolution. This makes an explicit form of the resolution hard to track! We illustrate with an example.

Example 3.4
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be the free operad discussed in Example
3.3
, generated by the multigraph (which encodes a tree) depicted as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

Let us compute the mapping space
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}(a_{1},a_{2};c)
.

We have four classes of operation vectors with inputs
a_{1},a_{2}
and output
c
:

\begin{bmatrix}g\circ\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&g\circ\binom{f_{1}}{1}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\circ\binom{1}{f_{2}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}

We also have the following configuration of
1
-simplices between the above
0
-simplices:

\begin{bmatrix}g\circ\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&g\circ\binom{f_{1}}{1}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\circ\binom{1}{f_{2}}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&g\circ\binom{f_{1}}{1}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\circ\binom{1}{f_{2}}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix}&\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
1\end{bmatrix}&\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

Notice that the edges on the top, the right and the diagonal symbolically behave as in the free resolution for categories, in the sense that the faces are apparent. For the edge on the bottom, the face
d_{1}
is clear, but the face
d_{0}
is correctly depicted by virtue of the relation (OU) since we have

d_{0}\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
1\end{bmatrix}&\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{1}&\binom{1}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}

Similarly for the edge on the right.

Finally, we do have
2
-simplices which fill the top and bottom triangle, written respectively

\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix}&\begin{bmatrix}\binom{f_{1}}{1}&g\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
1\end{bmatrix}&\begin{bmatrix}\binom{1}{f_{2}}&g\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

as expected. We conclude that

FU_{*}\mathscr{P}(a_{1},a_{2};c)\cong\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}

Note
.

The above computation agrees with the simplicial version of the
W
-construction in
[
26
, Section 2.7.7]
. In the language of trees, the mapping space between
(a_{1},a_{2})
and
c
in the above example is taken by definition to be the product over inner leaves between
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
and
c
of copies of
\Delta^{1}
. Since we have two inner leaves
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
, the mapping space is
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
. We believe the two constructions are isomorphic, but here we only prove they have the same homotopy type.

While the mapping spaces in the free resolution
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
, as the above example indicates, may be hard to describe in explicit terms, this resolution possesses a number of good properties by virtue of the manner in which it is constructed.

Proposition 3.2
.

The morphism
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}
induced by composing in
\mathscr{P}
is a weak equivalence of simplicial operads, where
\mathscr{P}
is regarded as discrete.

Proof.

We have an augmentation with extra-degeneracies
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}\dashrightarrow\mathscr{P}
, obtained exactly as in the classical case of categories (see appendices
A
and
B
). The augmentation induces a morphism of simplicial operads
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}
(regarding
\mathscr{P}
as discrete) which is identity on colors. Moreover, since we can carry the original augmentation to operation spaces, meaning that for all sequences of colors
{\underline{a}}
and color
b
of
\mathscr{P}
we have an augmentation of simplicial sets
(FU_{*}\mathscr{P})({\underline{a}},b)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{P}({\underline{a}},b)
with extra degeneracies, we deduce that the induced map
(FU_{*}\mathscr{P})({\underline{a}},b)\to\mathscr{P}({\underline{a}},b)
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets.

∎

Remark 3.3

(Operad valued simplicial computads and cofibrancy)
.

Using the notion of multigraph and the free operad construction, we can also implement the classical idea of simplicial computad (
[
38
, Section 16.2]
) in the context of operads. We sketch this idea here, but do not proceed with proofs.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad. An operation
h\in\text{Op}(\mathscr{P})
is said to be
atomic
if, in case
h=g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
for some operations
g,f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
, either
g
is an identity or
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
are all identities. We say that an operad
\mathscr{P}
is
free
if it is freely generated by the multigraph of its atomic operations.

Next, we may define an
operad valued simplicial computad
to be a discrete simplicial object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}

such that

-

For each
[n]\in\Delta
,
\mathscr{A}_{n}
is free.

-

Degeneracies of atomic operations are atomic.

We believe it is true that operad-valued simplicial computads are the cofibrant objects in
\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
. This would immediately imply that, for every operad
\mathscr{P}
, the simplicial operad
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
is cofibrant and hence it serves as a cofibrant replacement.

We leave the above claim without proof for two reasons. First, it should follow from well-known arguments (see
[
38
, Lemma 16.2.2]
) which rely on niceness properties of the model structure which
\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
possesses. Second, we do not use this characterization of cofibrant objects in our proofs. In fact, we will introduce another model for the cofibrant replacement of an operad in Section
5
which has nicer features.

We end with a proposition about the relationship between
\mathscr{L}
and
FU_{*}
. Notice that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU_{*}}

does not commute. In fact, the above square is far from being commutative. For an operad
\mathscr{P}
, the resolution
FU_{*}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})
has more simplices in the mapping spaces compared to the simplicial category
\mathscr{L}FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
. Nonetheless, the aforementioned simplicial categories have the same homotopy type.

Proposition 3.3
.

There is a natural weak equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof.

For a fixed operad
\mathscr{P}
we define a simplicial functor
D:FU_{*}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}(FU_{*}\mathscr{P})
as follows. On the level of
0
-simplices in the mapping spaces, notice that we do have a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{0}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}FU(\mathscr{P})}

Morphisms in
FU(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})
are represented by chains of morphisms in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
, which are just operation matrices in
\mathscr{P}
. By splitting these matrices into rows, we obtain a list of representatives for morphisms in
\mathscr{L}FU(\mathscr{P})
. It is not difficult to check that we obtain a well-defined functor
D_{0}
with such a splitting process.
Notice that, by construction, we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FU(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}FU(\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\epsilon_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{0}}

Moreover,
D_{0}
is natural in
\mathscr{P}
, so that we may regard it as a natural transformation
D_{0}:FU\mathscr{L}\Rightarrow\mathscr{L}FU
.

On the next iteration of both resolutions, we define the functor
D_{1}
as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFU(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(\mathscr{L}FU\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}(FUFU\mathscr{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU(D_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{0}}

In higher dimension, we define
D_{n}:FU^{(n)}\to\mathscr{L}(FU^{(n)}\mathscr{P})
recursively by “pulling
\mathscr{L}
from the left to the right step by step”.
In conclusion, we obtain a morphism of simplicial categories
D:FU_{*}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}FU_{*}(\mathscr{P})
which is natural in
\mathscr{P}
.

By construction, we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FU_{*}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}FU_{*}(\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

The weak equivalence on the left is the structure map of the resolution of
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
as a category. The map on the right is obtained by applying
\mathscr{L}
to the structure map
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}
of the resolution for
\mathscr{P}
. The latter is a weak equivalence by Proposition
3.2
. Thus, the morphism displayed in the above diagram is a weak equivalence since
\mathscr{L}
preserves weak equivalences (Lemma
2.4
). We conclude that
D
is also a weak equivalence by the two-out-of-three property of weak equivalences.
∎

3.4
Nerves and quasi-operads

Now we fill in the details for the sketch of the nerve construction provided in the introduction. We begin by encoding leveled trees as multigraphs.

Notation 3.2

(Simplices of
N(\Delta_{+})
)
.

We denote elements of
N(\Delta_{+})
by Greek letters
\alpha
,
\beta
, …. Given
\alpha:\Delta^{n}\to\Delta_{+}
, we denote by
A_{i}
the set
\alpha(i)
and by
\alpha_{i}:A_{i-1}\to A_{i}
the image under
\alpha
of the arrow
(i-1)\to i
in
\Delta
,
1\leq i\leq n
, so that
\alpha
is depicted as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}

We denote by
\alpha_{i,j}:A_{i}\to A_{j}
the image of the arrow
i\to j
in
\Delta^{n}
, for all
0\leq i\leq j\leq n
.

Similarly, for a simplex
\beta
, we use the symbols
B_{i}
,
\beta_{i}
and
\beta_{i,j}
.

Construction 3.2

(Key free operads)
.

Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
.
We define (the non-reflexive) multigraph
M_{\alpha}
with sets of simplices

M_{\alpha,0}=\coprod_{i=0}^{n}A_{i}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ M_{\alpha,1}=\coprod_{i=1}^{n}\{p_{i}^{(a)}\ :\ a\in A_{i}\}

Let
d_{0}p_{i}^{(a)}=a
and
d_{1}p_{i}^{(a)}=\alpha_{i}^{-1}(a)
in case
a\in A_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
. The sets
A_{i}
are ordered, so we may regard the preimage
\alpha_{i}^{-1}(a)
as a sequence in
M_{\alpha,0}
. The simplicial identities are clearly satisfied.

We define the operad

T_{\alpha}=FM_{\alpha}

In particular, given indices
i\leq j
, and
a\in A_{j}
we have an operation

p_{i,j}^{(a)}:\alpha_{i,j}^{-1}(a)\to a

in
T_{\alpha}
obtained by the evident chain of generating operations in
M_{\alpha}
. These operations satisfy

p_{i,j}^{(a)}\circ\left(p_{j,k}^{(b)}\right)_{b\in\alpha_{i,j}^{-1}(a)}=p_{i,k}^{(a)}

for all indices
i\leq j\leq k
. Under this notation, we have
1_{a}=p_{i,i}^{(a)}
.

Remark 3.4
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a multigraph. Following up on Note
Note
, we can say that an operation vector in
\mathscr{M}
is a morphism of multigraphs
M_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{M}
for some rooted simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
, while allowing
\alpha
to be a general simplex recovers the notion of operation matrix.

Lemma 3.3
.

The construction
\alpha\mapsto T_{\alpha}
is functorial in the simplex category, i.e. we have a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{*}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int N(\Delta_{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
,
\theta:[k]\to[n]
be a morphism in
\Delta
and say
\beta=\theta^{*}\alpha
. We specify the induced morphism of operads
\lambda_{\theta}:T_{\beta}\to T_{\alpha}
by saying that on the generating multigraph
M_{\beta}
we have

\lambda_{\theta}(p_{i}^{(b)})=p_{\theta(i-1),\theta(i)}^{(b)}

for all
i=1,\dots,k-1
and
b\in B_{i}
. This construction clearly respects composition.

∎

Notation 3.3
.

For this section, we retain the notation
\lambda_{\theta}
in the proof of the above lemma.

Remark 3.5
.

The morphisms
\lambda_{\theta}
can be interpreted as follows. In case
\theta=d_{0}
or
\theta=d_{n}
, the induced morphism
\lambda_{\theta}:T_{d_{i}\alpha}\to T_{\alpha}
is the evident inclusion. In case
\theta=d_{i}
is an inner face map, the induced morphism
\lambda_{d_{i}}:T_{d_{i}\alpha}\to T_{\alpha}
is obtained by composing in the
i
-th level of the operations. In case
\theta=s_{i}
is a degeneracy map, the induced morphism
\lambda_{s_{i}}:T_{s_{i}\alpha}\to T_{\alpha}
is obtained by mapping the
i
-th level of operations in the domain to identities in the codomain.

For each
[n]\in\Delta
, the set
N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
has the structure of a monoid given by ordinal sum. To be more precise, for
\alpha,\beta\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
we define

(\alpha\amalg\beta)(n)=A_{n}\amalg B_{n}.

Lemma 3.4
.

For all
[n]\in\Delta
and
\alpha,\beta\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
we have
T_{\alpha\amalg\beta}\cong T_{\alpha}\amalg T_{\beta}
.

Proof.

This is clear. In fact, on the level of multigraphs we have
M_{\alpha\amalg\beta}\cong M_{\alpha}\amalg M_{\beta}
.
∎

One reason the operads
T_{\alpha}
are key is the following proposition.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad. There is bijection

N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{n}\cong\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\alpha},\mathscr{P})

for all
[n]\in\Delta
which is natural in
\mathscr{P}
and
[n]
.

Proof.

Consider an
n
-simplex in
N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{n}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underline{a}}^{(0)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underline{a}}^{(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\underline{a}}^{(n)}}}

Each object in the chain is a sequence
{\underline{a}}^{(i)}:A_{i}\to\text{Col}(\mathscr{P})
,
A_{i}\in\Delta_{+}
, and underlying each morphism in the chain there is a morphism
\alpha_{i}:A_{i-1}\to A_{i}
in
\Delta_{+}
. This determines and is determined by specifying an
n
-simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
. The rest of the data determines and is determined by a morphism of multigraphs
M_{\alpha}\to U\mathscr{P}
, or equivalently a morphism of operads
T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P}
.

Naturality in
\mathscr{P}
is evident. By naturality in
[n]
we mean that for all
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta
the following square commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\alpha},\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\coprod_{\beta\in N(\Delta_{+})_{k}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\beta},\mathscr{P})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\lambda_{\theta}^{*}}

∎

Definition 3.3

(Rooted simplex)
.

A simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
is called rooted if
A_{n}\cong[0]
. We denote by
N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
the set of rooted
n
-simplices in
N(\Delta_{+})
.

The multigraphs
M_{\alpha}
are key in understanding the simplicial aspects of operad theory, since we can perform simplicial operations on simplices of
\Delta_{+}
. For a rooted simplex
\alpha
, the multigraph
M_{\alpha}
encodes precisely the data for a rooted tree. While in general,
M_{\alpha}
records a forest consisting of a sequence of rooted trees. This sequencing is precisely what generates listings in our approach.

Construction 3.3

(Root restriction)
.

Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
and
a\in A_{n}
. The rooted restriction of
\alpha
at
a
is defined to be the simplex
\alpha_{a}\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
given by pulling back the chain of morphisms in
\alpha
along the inclusion
\{a\}\subseteq A_{n}
as indicated in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{a}:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0,a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n-1,a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{a\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n-1}}

Lemma 3.5

(Rooted decomposition)
.

For all
[n]\in\Delta
, level-wise coproduct of ordered sets exhibits
N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
as a free monoid generated by the set
N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
, i.e.

N(\Delta_{+})_{n}\cong\mathscr{L}N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}

Proof.

For each
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
we have

\alpha=\coprod_{a\in A_{n}}\alpha_{a}

This representation is unique, given that
A_{n}
is ordered.
∎

Example 3.5

(
N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
as a simplicial list)
.

The nerve
N(\Delta_{+})
is a simplicial set by construction, but restricting to sets of rooted simplices breaks the simplicial structure since some simplicial operations may cut the root of the trees these simplices encode. However, we can assemble the sets of rooted simplices
N(\Delta_{+})^{root}_{n}
,
[n]\in\Delta
, in a simplicial list

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\Delta_{+})^{root}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{List}}

The action of a morphism
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta
on a rooted simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
is given by the rooted decomposition of the simplex
\theta^{*}\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{k}
, i.e.

\theta^{*}(\alpha)=((\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a})_{a\in A_{\theta((k)}}

In fact, this is an operadic simplicial list since, if
\theta(k)=n
, we will have
A_{\theta(k)}=A_{n}\cong[0]
and the output sequence on the right will have a single element.

Construction 3.4

(List nerves of operads)
.

For an operad
\mathscr{P}
we construct the
list nerve

N^{l}\mathscr{P}\in\mathsf{sList}
with set of
n
-simplices given by

N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{n}=\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\alpha},\mathscr{P})

for all
[n]\in\Delta
.

Let
\theta:[k]\to[n]
be a morphism in
\Delta
,
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
and
x:T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P}
be an
n
-simplex in the list nerve of
\mathscr{P}
. The simplex
\theta^{*}\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{k}
has a rooted decomposition
\theta^{*}\alpha=\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in A_{\theta(k)}}(\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a}
(Lemma
3.5
), which induces an isomorphism

T_{\alpha}\cong\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in A_{\theta(k)}}T_{(\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a}}

(Lemma
3.4
). For
a\in A_{\theta(k)}
, let
(\theta,a)^{*}x\in N(\mathscr{P})_{k}
be the
k
-simplex represented by the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\theta,a)^{*}x}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{(\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in A_{\theta(k)}}T_{(\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a}}\cong T_{\theta^{*}\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

Finally, we define the list
\theta^{*}x\in\mathscr{L}N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{k}
as

\theta^{*}x=((\theta,a)^{*}x)_{a\in A_{\theta(k)}}

The simplicial identities are evident. Also, the simplicial list
N^{l}(\mathscr{P})
is clearly operadic.

The nerve construction extends to a fully-faithful functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{l}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}}

This is easy to check, but we note that fully-faithfulness is due to the fact that morphisms between simplicial lists are defined to have functional components.

Example 3.6
.

We have an isomorphism of simplicial lists

N^{l}\mathsf{Assoc}\cong N(\Delta_{+})^{root}

Indeed, for each
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
there is a unique
\alpha
-shaped simplex
T_{\alpha}\to\mathsf{Assoc}
. Therefore we have
(N^{l}\mathsf{Assoc})_{n}\cong N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

The following squares commute (up to isomorphism)

\hbox to99.26pt{\vbox to55.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 49.63034pt\lower-28.87845pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.65279pt}{-25.41245pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.29 -35.16)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Proof.

The square on the left says that the nerve construction for operads extends the ordinary nerve construction for categories. This is clear, since, for a category
\mathscr{C}
and
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
, there is a morphism
T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{C}
if and only if
A_{i}\cong[0]
for all
i
. The latter implies that
T_{\alpha}\cong\Delta^{n}
(regarding
\Delta^{n}
as a category and subsequently as an operad with unary operations only). Hence, the operadic nerve of
\mathscr{C}
gives back the categorical nerve.

The square on the right says that the functor
\mathscr{L}:\mathsf{sList}\to\mathsf{sSet}
extends the monoidal envelope functor (in particular, this justifies notation). Proposition
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
provides us respectively the isomorphisms
N(\Delta_{+})_{n}\cong\mathscr{L}(N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root})
and
N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{n}\cong\amalg_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\alpha},\mathscr{P})
. From this we deduce the sequence of equalities

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(N^{l}\mathscr{P})_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}(N^{l}(\mathscr{P})_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}\left(\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\alpha},\mathscr{P})\right)

\displaystyle\cong\coprod_{(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})\in\mathscr{L}(N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root})}\mathsf{Op}(\amalg_{i}T_{\alpha_{i}},\mathscr{P})

\displaystyle\cong\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}}\mathsf{Op}(T_{\alpha},\mathscr{P})

\displaystyle\cong N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{n}

where the isomorphism of the last line is natural in
\mathscr{P}
and compatible with
simplicial identities by the naturality in
[n]
, hence, the commutation of the
square up to natural isomorphism.
∎

Theorem 3.1

(Nerve Theorem)
.

A simplicial list
X
is (isomorphic to the list nerve of) an operad if and only if
X
is operadic and
\mathscr{L}X
is (isomorphic to the nerve of) a category.

Proof.

One direction is clear. For an operad
\mathscr{P}
, the nerve
N^{l}\mathscr{P}
is operadic by construction and we have
\mathscr{L}N^{l}(\mathscr{P})\cong N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})
by the previous proposition.

For the converse, let
X\in\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
such that
\mathscr{L}X\cong N(\mathscr{C})
for some category
\mathscr{C}
. We may define an operad
\mathscr{P}_{X}
as follows:

-

The set of colors is given by
X_{0}
.

-

The set of operations is given by
X_{1}
.

-

Identity operations are given by degeneracies. This is well-defined since
s_{0}:X_{0}\to X_{1}
is a function by
X
being operadic.

-

Composition is given by the composition operation in
\mathscr{C}
, coupled with the fact that
X
is operadic.

To formalize the last item somewhat, given a
1
-simplex
g:{\underline{b}}\to c
and a sequence of
1
-simplices
\underline{f}=(f_{i}:{\underline{a}}_{i}\to b_{i})_{i}\in\mathscr{L}X_{1}
which is composable with
g
, their composite exists in
\mathscr{C}
(under the identification
\mathscr{L}X\cong\mathscr{C}
). A priory this only gives us back a sequence
g\circ\underline{f}=\underline{h}\in\mathscr{L}X_{1}
. This sequence is forced to be a singleton (and hence an element of
X_{1}
) by the fact that
X
is operadic. Indeed, we do have that
d_{0}\underline{h}=d_{0}g
by the simplicial identities and since
d_{0}g=c
, we conclude that
\underline{h}=h\in X_{1}
. In similar fashion, we may argue that associativity and unitality of composition is inherited by
\mathscr{C}
.

It is also easy to check that
X\cong N^{l}(\mathscr{P}_{X})
. An
n
-simplex
x\in X_{n}
, when regarded as an element
(x)\in N^{l}(\mathscr{L}X)_{n}
, is uniquely determined by its spine. The latter determines and is determined by a choice of
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
and a morphism
M_{\alpha}\to U\mathscr{P}_{X}
. Note that since
X
is operadic, the last vertex of any simplex
x\in X
is a singleton sequence in
X_{0}
. This forces the corresponding
\alpha
to be rooted.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The fact that
\mathscr{L}X
is a category can be transposed as a strict inner horn filling condition in
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
, i.e. if
\Lambda_{i}^{n}\subseteq\Delta^{n}
is a horn inclusion,
0<i<n
, then any listing
\Lambda_{i}^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X
extends uniquely to a listing
\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda_{i}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\ \text{listing}}

The condition of
X
being operadic ensures that composition of operations does not behave erratically, but provides well-defined operations (rather than sequences) where it is reasonable to expect so. We obtain our model for
\infty
-operads by requiring the inner horn filling condition to be weak, i.e. by removing the uniqueness condition.

Definition 3.4

(Quasi-operad)
.

A
quasi-operad
is an operadic simplicial list
P
such that the simplicial set
\mathscr{L}P
is an quasi-category.

Example 3.7
.

For an operad
\mathscr{P}
, the simplicial list
N^{l}(\mathscr{P})
is a quasi-operad.

Note
.

As trivial as it is from the nerve theorem, the above example is important because it tells us that our model for
\infty
-operads captures the theory
of
1
-operads. In Section
5.2
we provide another class of examples coming from operads enriched in spaces.

Even without this promise, we can see how regarding quasi-operads as
\infty
-operads is justified by the well-established quasi-categorical model for
\infty
-categories. An quasi-operad
P
will encode the following:

-

A set of colors given by
P_{0}
.

-

A set of operations given by the set
P_{1}
, which have a sequence of colors as source and a single color as target.

-

A composition operation which is well-defined, unital and associative up to coherent homotopy recorded in the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{L}P
, which is well-behaved by virtue of
\mathscr{P}
being operadic as a simplicial list.

A full justification for quasi-operads to be a model for
\infty
-operads would be to construct a model structure on
\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
with fibrant objects being quasi-operads and which is Quillen equivalent to the model structure on simplicial operads. We do not pursue this line in this paper, but intend to do so in a sequel paper.

Note

(Compositional networks)
.

As mentioned in the introduction, the nerve theorem for operads leads us to consider a more general type of structure which we named
compositional network
in Definition
1.4
. These are (not neccesarily operadic) simplicial lists
X
such that
\mathscr{L}X
is (the nerve of) a category.

We could read the nerve theorem for operads to say that operads are operadic compositional networks. Similarly, one can easily imagine that properads may be identified with properadic compositional networks. At this point one can only wonder whether there are algebraic structures of interest which can be modeled by more general compositional networks. The terminology is inspired by current developments in applied category theory, where networks of composable operations (mostly expressed in the language of monoidal categories) play a prominent role (see
[
17
]
for many examples).

4
The structure of simplicial lists

4.1
The perfect-function factorization

Definition 4.1
.

A listing
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}X
is said to be
perfect
if

-

Each element
x\in X
appears as a term in
u(a)
for a unique
a\in A
.

-

For each
a\in A
, the sequence
u(a)
has as terms distinct elements of
X
.

We say that a listing
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}X
between simplicial lists is
perfect
if it is so pointwise, i.e. if the listing
u_{n}:A_{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X_{n}
is perfect for all
n\geq 0
.

Example 4.1
.

We depict a few examples of listings
\{a,b\}{\mathrel{\to}}\{x,y,z,w\}
