Example 4.2
.

A function is perfect if and only if it is an isomorphism.

Proposition 4.1
.

Every listing
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}X
may be factored as a perfect listing
{u}:A{\mathrel{\to}}U
followed by a function
f_{u}:U\to X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{u}}

Moreover, the choice of
U
is unique (up to isomorphism).

Proof.

Let
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}X
be a listing. Define the set

U=\left\{(a,i):a\in A,1\leq i\leq|u(a)|\right\}

where
|u(a)|
indicates the length of the sequence
u(a)
for
a\in A
. There is a listing
\tilde{u}:A{\mathrel{\to}}U
,
a\mapsto((a,1),\dots,(a,|u(a)|))
which is perfect and a function
f_{u}:U\to X
,
(a,i)\mapsto u(a)_{i}
where
u(a)_{i}
is the
i
-th term of the sequence
u(a)
. This provides the perfect-function factorization.

In case of another perfect-function factorization of
u
, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{u}^{\prime}}

notice that we must have
|\tilde{u}^{\prime}(a)|=|u(a)|=|\tilde{u}(a)|
for all
a\in A
. Thus, there is a well-defined map
U\to U^{\prime}
,
(a,i)\mapsto\tilde{u}^{\prime}(a)_{i}
, which is a bijection since
\tilde{u}^{\prime}
is a perfect listing.
∎

For the rest of the paper, we refer to the above factorization as
the perfect-function
factorization of a listing.

Note
.

A perfect listing
A{\mathrel{\to}}X
can be identified with a function
X\to A
whose fibres are ordered. The above lemma is trivial from this angle, since every listing can be presented as a span
A\leftarrow U\to X
where the fibres of
U\to A
are ordered.

Proposition 4.2
.

The perfect-function factorization is functorial, in the sense that it extends into a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{List}^{\Delta^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{List}^{\Delta^{1}}\times_{\mathsf{List}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Set}^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A{\mathrel{\to}^{u}}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A{\mathrel{\to}^{\tilde{u}}}U,U\xrightarrow{f_{u}}X)}

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{Set}^{\Delta^{1}}}
is the subcategory of
\mathsf{List}^{\Delta^{1}}
consisting of functions.

Proof.

We rephrase the statement of the proposition as follows. Given the solid arrow diagram of listings and functions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{v}}

which depicts listings
u
and
v
and a morphism
(p,q):u\rightarrow v
in
\mathsf{List}^{\Delta^{1}}
(i.e. a commutative square in
\mathsf{List}
), there is a unique listing ( the dotted arrow)
r:U{\mathrel{\to}}V
which makes the diagram commute.

Using notation from the proof of the previous proposition, for
(a,i)\in U
, we define

r(a,i)=\{(b,j):b\in p(a)\ \text{and}\ v(b)_{j}\in q(u(a)_{i})\}

The above set, in lexicographic order, represents a sequence in
V
. It is not difficult to check that
r
commutes with the solid arrows in the above diagram.
∎

Corollary 4.1
.

Every listing
u:A{\mathrel{\to}}X
between simplicial lists factors functorially as a perfect listing
\tilde{u}:A{\mathrel{\to}}U
followed by a functional morphism
f_{u}:U\to X
. In particular, the simplicial list
U
is unique up to unique isomorphism.

Proof.

This follows by applying the perfect-function factorization pointwise and then the above proposition. More precisely, for each
[n]\in\Delta
the listing
u_{n}:A_{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X_{n}
has a perfect-function factorization
A_{n}{\mathrel{\to}^{\tilde{u}_{n}}}U_{n}\xrightarrow{(f_{u})_{n}}X
and for each
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta
the commutative square in
\mathsf{List}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

induces a listing
\theta^{*}:U_{n}{\mathrel{\to}}U_{k}
(the dotted arrow in terms of the diagram in the proof of the previous proposition).
Canonical choice follows from the fact that invertible listings are isomorphisms.
∎

4.2
Fundamental simplicial lists

A nice feature of simplicial sets is that for a simplicial set
X
, an
n
-simplex
x\in X_{n}
is represented by a morphism
x:\Delta^{n}\to X
. Moreover, each simplicial set
X
is the colimit of its simplices. These facts are consequences of the category of simplicial sets being a presheaf category, but they also capture the geometric aspects of simplex formations. We develop a similar understanding for simplicial lists.

Let
X\in\mathsf{sList}
. An
n
-simplex
x\in X_{n}
, when regarded as a singleton sequence
(x)\in\mathscr{L}X_{n}
, is represented by a listing
(x):\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X
. By applying the perfect-function factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces perfect}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces function}

we may represent
x
functionally via a morphism
U_{x}\to X
for some simplicial list
U_{x}
which is determined up to unique isomorphism. We may say that the simplex
x
is of
shape

U_{x}
. This way, we are lead to the following class of simplicial lists which serve as candidate shapes for simplices.

Definition 4.2

(Fundamental simplicial list)
.

A simplicial list
U
is said to be
fundamental
if it is equipped with a perfect listing
u:\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}U
for some
n\geq 0
, such that
u(1_{[n]})
is a singleton sequence. In this case we identify
u\in U_{n}
as an
n
-simplex referred to as the
fundamental
n
-simplex
of
U
.

We denote the collection of fundamental simplicial lists indexed by a perfect listing from
\Delta^{n}
by
\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
and by
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathsf{sList}
the full subcategory of simplicial lists spanned by the fundamental ones.

Notation 4.1
.

We write
(U,u)\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
to indicate that
U
is a fundamental simplicial list indexed by a perfect listing from
\Delta^{n}
with fundamental simplex
u
.

Example 4.3
.

For all
n\geq 0
, the simplicial set
\Delta^{n}
is a fundamental simplicial list via the identity morphism. The fundamental
n
-simplex is the identity function
1_{[n]}\in\Delta^{n}_{n}
.

Being indexed by objects of
\Delta
, fundamental simplicial lists inherit a number of pleasant properties.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(U,u)\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
,
n\geq 0
.

(i)

Each simplex
u^{\prime}\in U_{k}
,
k\geq 0
, appears exactly once in sequence
\theta^{*}u
for a unique simplicial operation
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta
.

(ii)

For a simplicial list
X
, a morphism
U\to X
is uniquely determined by the image of
u
.

(iii)

Let
(V,v)\in\mathscr{F}^{(k)}
,
k\geq 0
. A morphism
\gamma:V\to U
in
\mathscr{F}
is uniquely determined by a simplicial operation
\theta:[k]\to[n]
and an index
i
such that
\gamma(v)=(\theta^{*}u)_{i}
.

Proof.

(i)

Since
u:\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}U
is perfect, at the
k
-th level the listing between sets
u_{k}:\Delta^{n}_{k}{\mathrel{\to}}U_{k}
is perfect. Thus, given a simplex
u^{\prime}\in U_{k}
there is a unique
\theta:[k]\to[n]
and index
i
such that
u^{\prime}
is the
i
-th term of the sequence
\theta^{*}u
.

(ii)

Let
f:U\to X
be a morphism of simplicial lists and let
x=f(u)
. Since any other simplex
u^{\prime}\in U_{k}
is of the form
(\theta^{*}u)_{i}
by (i), it follows that
f(u^{\prime})=(\theta^{*}x)_{i}
in
X
as
f
is structure preserving. This means that the image of
u
determines the image of any other simplex of
U
under
f
.

(iii)

In case
(V,v)\in\mathscr{F}^{(k)}
and
\gamma:V\to U
is a morphism of simplicial lists, by (ii), it is uniquely determined by the image of the fundamental simplex
\gamma(v)\in U_{k}
. By (i),
\gamma(v)=(\theta^{*}u)_{i}
for some
\theta:[k]\to[n]
and index
i
.

∎

Notation 4.2
.

For
(U,u)\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
and
(V,v)\in\mathscr{F}^{(k)}

we denote by
\gamma_{\theta,i}:V\to U
the unique morphism in
\mathscr{F}
such that
\gamma_{\theta,i}(v)=(\theta^{*}u)_{i}
. Here,
\theta:[k]\to[n]
is a morphism in
\Delta
and
i
is an indexing number.

Remark 4.1
.

It follows from the above lemma that in case two fundamental simplicial lists are isomorphic, then this isomorphism is unique. Indeed, an invertible morphism
(U,u)\to(V,v)
has to map
u\mapsto v
. This justifies the following abuse: we identify
\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
with the set of isomorphism classes of fundamental simplicial list indexed by
\Delta^{n}
and thus regard
\mathscr{F}
to be skeletal. This is mostly to avoid set-theoretic issues, as for instance in the following proposition.

As mentioned in the beginning of the section, fundamental simplicial lists are designed to be
simplex classifiers
.

Proposition 4.3

(Classification of simplices)
.

Let
X
be a simplicial list. There is a natural isomorphism

X_{n}\cong\coprod_{U\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}}\mathsf{sList}(U,X)

Proof.

Let
x\in X_{n}
. As pointed out previously, by regarding
(x)\in\mathscr{L}X_{n}
as a singleton sequence, we may present it with a listing
(x):\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}X
. This listing factors as a perfect listing
u_{x}:\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}U_{x}
followed by a function
f_{x}:U\to X
for a unique
U_{x}
. Notice that
u_{x}(1_{[n]})
has to be a singleton sequence in
\mathscr{L}(U_{x})_{n}
since
f_{x}\circ u_{x}
classifies a singleton sequence in
\mathscr{L}X_{n}
and
f_{x}
is a function pointwise. Thus,
U_{x}\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
and we have a well-defined mapping
x\mapsto(f_{x}:U_{x}\to X)
.

On the other hand, given
u:\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}U
in
\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
and a map
f:U\to X
, we obtain a simplex
f(u)\in X_{n}
(Lemma
4.1
(ii)). This gives us a mapping
(f:U\to X)\mapsto f(u)
.

By the uniqueness of the perfect-function factorization, these mappings are inverses of each other. Naturality is clear.
∎

Let
X
be a simplicial list. In light of the above, we can see that, besides the fact that simplices
x\in X_{n}
are represented by morphisms
U\to X
,
u\mapsto x
from fundamental simplicial lists, a simplicial relation in
X
between simplices of the form
y=(\theta^{*}x)_{i}
is represented by a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\theta,i}}

Commutative triangles as a above are precisely the morphisms in the comma category
(\mathscr{F}\downarrow X)
. We regard the latter as the
category of simplices
of
X
and denote it by
\mathsf{Spx}(X)
. In other words,
\mathsf{Spx}(X)
is defined by saying that:

-

Simplices
x\in X_{n}
,
n\geq 0
, are the objects.

-

There is a morphism
y\to x
between
x\in X_{n}
and
y\in X_{k}
in case of a simplicial relation
y=(\theta^{*}x)_{i}
.

There is a natural forgetful functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{X}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Spx}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\to X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

Proposition 4.4

(Colimit of simplices)
.

Every simplicial list
X
is a colimit of its simplices, i.e. there is an isomorphism

X\cong\displaystyle\colim_{U\to X}U

The above proposition can be derived as a corollary of (the proof) of the following key theorem of this paper, so we present its proof afterwards.

Theorem 4.1

(Presheaf Theorem)
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\mathsf{sList}\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\mathscr{F}^{op}}

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathsf{sList}
. We may form the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{X}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}(U,X)}

Moreover,
X\mapsto H_{X}
gives us a functor
H_{\bullet}:\mathsf{sList}\to\mathsf{Set}^{\mathscr{F}^{op}}
.

Let
G:\mathscr{F}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
be a presheaf. We defıne a simplicial list

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{G}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{List}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[n\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{U\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}}G(U)}

If we denote
G(\gamma_{\theta,i})=G_{\theta,i}
, given
x\in G(U)
, we define
(\theta^{*}x)_{i}=G_{\theta,i}(x)
. The mapping
G\mapsto Y_{G}
gives rise to a functor
Y_{\bullet}:\mathsf{Set}^{\mathscr{F}^{op}}\to\mathsf{sList}
.

Let us see that the above functors are weak inverses of each other. One direction is easy. For
X\in\mathsf{sList}
, the simplicial list
Y_{H_{X}}
has as
n
-simplices the set
\displaystyle\coprod_{U\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}}\mathsf{sList}(U,X)
. The latter is naturally isomorphic to
X_{n}
(Proposition
3.4
).

For the other direction, let
G:\mathscr{F}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
be a presheaf. The presheaf
H_{Y_{G}}
assigns to an object
U\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}
the set
\mathsf{sList}(U,Y_{G})
. But, every simplex of
Y_{G}
which belongs to the set
G(U)
is
U
-shaped. This is so because if we are given
y\in G(U)
, we can form a morphism of simplicial lists
\eta_{y}:U\to Y_{G}
,
u\mapsto y
. This much information is enough to construct
\eta_{y}
, since given a simplex
\gamma_{\theta,i}:V\to U
in
U
(Lemma
4.1
(iii)) there is a structure function
G_{\theta,i}:G(U)\to G(V)
and we can declare
\eta_{y}(\gamma_{\theta,i})=G_{\theta,i}(y)
.

Thus, we obtain a bijection
G(U)\cong\mathsf{sList}(U,Y_{G})
,
y\mapsto\eta_{y}
, which is natural in
U
by construction and therefore results in a natural isomorphism
G\cong H_{Y_{G}}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2
.

The category
\mathsf{sList}
is complete and cocomplete.

Remark 4.2
.

Notice that the equivalence
\mathsf{sList}\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\mathscr{F}^{op}}
,
X\mapsto H_{X}
, constructed in the above proof is easily identified as the right adjoint induced by the inclusion
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathsf{sList}
via Yoneda extension.

Proof.

(of Proposition
4.4
)

Let
X
be a simplicial list. Following up on the above remark and using notation from the above theorem we have that

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\cong Y_{G_{X}}

\displaystyle\cong\displaystyle\colim_{U\in H_{X}(U)}U

\displaystyle\cong\displaystyle\colim_{U\to X}U

∎

Note

(Two notions of pointwise)
.

We have two notions of pointwise for simplicial lists: one from the simplicial structure and one from the presheaf structure. For example, limits of simplicial lists are computed pointwise in the presheaf structure, but they are not constructed simplicially pointwise. For instance, for simplicial lists
X
and
Y
we have that

(X\times Y)_{n}\cong\displaystyle\coprod_{U\in\mathscr{F}^{(n)}}(\mathsf{sList}(U,X)\times\mathsf{sList}(U,Y))

which means that the cartesian product in
\mathsf{sList}
is formed by pairs of simplices of the same shape. This is different from
X_{n}\times Y_{n}
. On the other hand, colimits are computed pointwise in both senses.

4.3
Types of simplicial lists

We have already encountered two types of simplicial lists, simplicial sets and operadic simplicial lists. Both are defined by requiring that a certain class of simplicial operations are functions. This leads us to consider the following generalisation.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
S\subseteq\text{Mor}(\Delta)
be a subset of morphisms in
\Delta
. We say that a simplicial list
X
is of type
S
if the structure map
\theta^{*}
of
X
is a function for all
\theta\in S
. We denote by
\mathsf{sList}_{S}\subseteq\mathsf{sList}
the full subcategory spanned by simplicial lists of type
S
.

Example 4.4
.

If
S=\text{Mor}(\Delta)
, then
\mathsf{sList}_{S}=\mathsf{sSet}
.

Example 4.5
.

If
S=\{\theta:[k]\to[n]:\theta(k)=n\}
, then
\mathsf{sList}_{S}=\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
S\subseteq\text{Mor}(\Delta)
. If there is a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathsf{sList}
and
Y
is of type
S
, then so is
X
.

Proof.

Let
\theta:[k]\to[n]
be a morphism in
S
. We have a commutative square in
\mathsf{List}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Since
Y
is of type
S
, the map on the bottom is a function. It follows by inspection that the map on the top is also a function.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
S\subseteq\text{Mor}(\Delta)
, and let
\mathscr{F}_{S}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
be the full subcategory spanned by objects of type
S
. Then, there is an equivalence of categories

\mathsf{sList}_{S}\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\mathscr{F}_{S}^{op}}

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathsf{sList}_{S}
. In the presheaf formation of
X
, by the previous lemma, the set
\mathsf{sList}(U,X)=\emptyset
unless
U\in\mathscr{F}_{S}
. Hence the desired conclusion.
∎

Notation 4.3
.

We denote by
\Upsilon
the category of fundamental operadic simplicial lists.

Corollary 4.3
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\Upsilon^{op}}

Remark 4.3
.

The reason we choose this level of generality, besides the easy derivation of the above important corollary, is that we believe interesting classes emerge by letting
S
vary. For instance, we would call a simplicial list
properadic
in case
S=\{\theta:[k]\to[n]:\theta(0)=0,\theta(k)=n\}
. Choosing the subset
S
of interest restricts the type of shapes from
\mathscr{F}
to
\mathscr{F}_{S}
according to the type of structure we are studying. In this vein, simplicial sets are the
categorical
simplicial lists.

4.4
Operadic simplicial lists

While it is important to know that
\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
is a presheaf category, in order to work properly with the presheaf structure we need a good understanding of the base
\Upsilon
. We construct and classify fundamental operadic simplicial lists.

Construction 4.1
.

Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
for some
n\geq 0
. Recall the usual convention of writing
\alpha
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}

and writing
\alpha_{i,j}:A_{i}\to A_{j}
for the appropriate composite of morphisms in the chain. We define the simplicial list
U_{\alpha}
with set of
k
-simplices given by

U_{\alpha,k}=\coprod_{\theta:[k]\to[n]}A_{\theta(k)}

In other words, a
k
-simplex in
U_{\alpha}
is a pair
(\theta,a)
, where
\theta:[k]\to[n]
is a morphism in
\Delta
and
a\in A_{\theta(k)}
, for all
k\geq 0
.

Let
\eta:[l]\to[k]
be a morphism in
\Delta
and
(\theta,a)
be a
k
-simplex in
U_{\alpha}
. The action of
\eta
on this simplex is given by

\eta^{*}(\theta,a)=\{(\theta\eta,b)\ :\ \alpha_{\theta\eta(l),\theta(k)}(b)=a\}

The order on the set on the right is inherited from
A_{\theta\eta(l)}
and we regard it as a sequence of
l
-simplices.
We see that
U_{\alpha}
is operadic, since, in case
\eta(l)=k
, the sequence on the right is just the singleton
(\theta\eta,a)
.

Moreover, there is an evident listing

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\alpha}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta:[k]\to[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\theta,a))_{a\in A_{\theta(k)}}}

which is perfect since, by construction, every simplex in
U_{\alpha}
appears exactly once as a term in an image sequence of the
u_{\alpha}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

An operadic simplicial list
U
is fundamental if and only if
U\cong U_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
. As demonstrated above, the listing
u_{\alpha}:\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}U_{\alpha}
is perfect for all
\alpha
. By definition, we have
u_{\alpha}(1_{[n]})\cong A_{n}
. Hence, this
U_{\alpha}
is fundamental just in case
A_{n}\cong[0]
, i.e. in case
\alpha
is rooted.

For the converse, let
u:\Delta^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}U
be a fundamental operadic simplicial list. Let us denote
u(i):A_{i}\to U_{0}
for some
A_{i}\in\Delta_{+}
,
0\leq i\leq n
. Since
u
is perfect, we have
U_{0}\cong\displaystyle\coprod_{i=0}^{n}A_{i}
.
Similarly, for
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta^{n}_{k}
, let us denote
u(\theta):A_{\theta}\to U_{k}
. Since
u
is perfect, we must have
U_{k}\cong\displaystyle\coprod_{\theta:[k]\to[n]}A_{\theta}
. In particular, since
U
is fundamental, we obtain
A_{n}\cong A_{1_{[n]}}\cong[0]
.

Let
t_{k}:[0]\to[k]
be the last vertex mapping
0\mapsto k
. We have a commutative square in
\mathsf{List}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{k}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{k}^{*}}

The listings on the top and bottom are perfect, while the structure map on the right is a function since
U
is operadic. Therefore, when restricting to a particular
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta^{n}_{k}
, we deduce that

A_{\theta}\cong A_{\theta(k)}

Now, we recover a simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}_{n}
with
\alpha(i)=A_{i}
.
In case
k=1
, we have a commutative square in
\mathsf{List}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{d}_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Every map
[1]\to[n]
amounts to choosing a pair
i,j\in[n]
with order
i\leq j
.
When restricting the above commutative square to a single element
(i,j)\in\Delta^{n}_{1}
, we have a commutative square in
\mathsf{List}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{(i,j)\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{(i,j)}\cong A_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{i\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{*}}

The listings on top and bottom are still perfect. Hence, we obtain a function

\alpha_{i,j}:A_{i}\to A_{j}

by declaring that
\alpha_{i,j}(a)=b
in case
a
appears as a term in the sequence
d_{1}^{*}(b)
. It is not difficult to show that the simplicial structure of
U
is precisely that of
U_{\alpha}
constructed above.

∎

Note

(
U_{\alpha}
as a partial operad)
.

The above combinatorics is somewhat involved, but there is an angle on the construction of
U_{\alpha}
we find explanatory. Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
. Combining a little jargon from operads and simplices, we may regard the set of
0
-simplices
\displaystyle\coprod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
as the
colors
of
U_{\alpha}
. The
operations
(
1
-simplices) are declared in the form
(\theta,a)
where
\theta:[1]\to[n]
and
a\in A_{\theta(1)}
.
The simplex
\theta
is just the choice of two indexes
i,j
with order
i\leq j
, while the specification of an element
a\in A_{j}
just says that this operation is of the form
\alpha_{i,j}^{-1}(a)\to a
.

In similar fashion,
2
-simplices are encoded in the form
((i,j,k),a)
, where
i,j,k
are indexes with order
i\leq j\leq k
and
a\in A_{k}
. These record a
partial composition
operation of the appropriate operations. We may think of the composition scheme as follows: an operation
g
may be composed with a sequence of operations
\underline{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{l})
if and only if
d_{0}g=d_{1}\underline{f}
and moreover all the terms of
\underline{f}
belong to the same level.

We will not formulate a notion of partial operad here, even though it is a useful heuristic in understanding the formation of
U_{\alpha}
and a thick version of it we will construct in the next section. Let us illustrate with an example.

Example 4.6
.

Let
\alpha
be the rooted
2
-simplex in
N(\Delta_{+})_{2}^{root}
which records the rooted tree

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}

The
0
-simplices, or colors, of
U_{\alpha}
are just
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1},b_{2},c
. The non-degenerate
1
-simplices, or operations, are
f_{1},f_{2},g
and moreover, we have another (non-depicted)
1
-simplex
h:(a_{1},a_{2})\to c
. We may think of
h
as the “composite”
g\circ(f_{1},f_{2})
. This composition is witnessed by the unique non-degenerate 2-simplex of
U_{\alpha}
(the fundamental one
u_{\alpha}
).

Notice that in
U_{\alpha}
we do not have other compositions, since composition is only allowed level-wise. For instance,
g\circ(f_{1},1_{b_{2}})
is not allowed since
f_{1}
is a term in the sequence
u_{\alpha}((0,1))
while
1_{b_{2}}
is a term in the sequence
u_{\alpha}((0,0))
, where
(0,1)
and
(1,1)
are the indexing
1
-simplices in
\Delta^{2}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

The category
\Upsilon
of fundamental operadic simplicial lists can be described as follows:

-

The objects are rooted simplices
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
,
n\geq 0
.

-

A morphism
(\theta,a):\beta\to\alpha
between
\beta\in\Upsilon^{(k)}
and
\alpha\in\Upsilon^{(n)}
consists of a map
\theta:[k]\to[n]
and an element
a\in A_{\theta(k)}
such that
\beta=(\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a}
, which means that
\beta
is the rooted restriction (Construction
3.3
) of
\theta^{*}\alpha
at
a
.

Proof.

The above proposition tells us that fundamental operadic simplicial lists are of the form
U_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
. Since
U_{\alpha}
is uniquely determined by
\alpha
, we may identify the objects of
\Upsilon
with rooted simplices of the category
\Delta_{+}
.

Let
\phi:U_{\beta}\to U_{\alpha}
be a morphism in
\Upsilon
.
By the general description of morphisms in
\mathscr{F}
(Lemma
4.1
),
\phi
is uniquely determined by the image of the fundamental
k
-simplex in
U_{\beta}
. By construction of
U_{\alpha}
, we may construct a
k
-simplex of shape
\beta
in
U_{\alpha}
if and only if we have a map
\theta:[k]\to[n]
such that
\beta=(\theta^{*}\alpha)_{a}
for some
a\in A_{\theta(k)}
. Hence, we may identify
\phi
with the pair
(\theta,a)
. It is easy to check that the above determine an isomorphism of categories.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

In
[
3
, Definition 2.4]
, Barwick constructs, for any operator category
\Phi
, a category
\Delta_{\Phi}
called the category of
\Phi
-
sequences
. Specializing to the case
\Phi=\Delta_{+}
, this category has the following description:

-

Objects are simplices
\alpha:\Delta^{n}\to\Delta_{+}
,
[n]\in\Delta
.

-

For
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
and
\beta\in N(\Delta_{+})_{k}
, a morphism
(\theta,\phi):\beta\to\alpha
consists of a map
\theta:[k]\to[n]
in
\Delta
and a natural transformation
\phi:\beta\to\alpha\circ\theta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For all
i\in[k]
, the component function
\phi_{i}:B_{i}\to A_{\theta(i)}
is an interval inclusion (meaning an inclusion such that the image consists of consecutive terms of the target).

(ii)

For all
i\leq j
in
[k]
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{\theta(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{\theta(j)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}

is a pullback square.

Notice that a morphism
(\theta,\phi):\beta\to\alpha
as above is completely determined by
\theta:[k]\to[n]
and an interval inclusion
B_{k}\subseteq A_{\theta(k)}
, as the rest of the simplex
\beta
and components of
\phi
are determined by condition
(ii)
.

In particular, in case
\beta
is rooted, the latter amounts to the choice of an element
a\in A_{\theta(k)}
. Thus, this notion of morphism coincides with the one between rooted simplices of
N(\Delta_{+})
described in the above proposition. This way, we see that
\Upsilon
can be interpreted as the full subcategory of
\Delta_{\Phi}
,
\Phi=\Delta_{+}
, spanned by rooted simplices.

This not only offers another description of morphisms in
\Upsilon
, but we may also conclude that Segal
\Upsilon
-spaces, meaning functors
\Upsilon^{op}\to\mathsf{Kan}
valued in Kan complexes (or any other model for spaces, or in the
\infty
-category of spaces) which satisfy a Segal condition (see for instance
[
3
, Definition 2.6]
or
[
11
, Definition 2.7]
), do model
\infty
-operads. For instance, an equivalence with Lurie’s model is established in
[
3
, Section 10]
.

We do not unpack the Segal conditions, although the reader can easily guess. In fact, we work in the quasi-categorical direction towards establishing a direct connection between simplicial operads and list quasi-operads. One reason for this is that simplicial lists and list quasi-operads are far easier to describe that Segal
\Upsilon
-spaces on the level of combinatorics. We intend to pursue a proper comparison in future work.

We use the description of
\Upsilon
in the statement of the above proposition, while we write

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\bullet}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}}

for the Yoneda embedding into
\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
.

Having done the combinatorial heavy-lifting, we may reinterpret the nerve functor
N^{l}:\mathsf{Op}\to\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
to be the right adjoint to the Yoneda extension of the functor
\alpha\mapsto T_{\alpha}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mapsto T_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}}

In fact, the presheaf structure indicates the nerve functor ought to be what it is, since the most evident operad one can construct out of a rooted simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
is the operad
T_{\alpha}
. Moreover, the Yoneda extension provides us with a
fundamental operad
, or
free operad
, functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}}

for free. The fact that
\tau_{1}U_{\alpha}=T_{\alpha}
, which follows by definition, is sensible when we regard
U_{\alpha}
as a partial operad (Note
Note
), in which case
T_{\alpha}
is the free operad which contains the missing composites.

Note
.

A major difference between the category
\Upsilon
and the category
\Delta
is the fact that the representables
U_{\alpha}
are not nerves of operads, while the simplicial sets
\Delta^{n}
are. This is not the case in dendroidal theory, where trees are fundamentally identified with the free operads they generate. On the other hand, the objects in our base
\Upsilon
do behave like simplices in many other ways and morphisms between them are tractable combinatorially and there is far less of them compared to the dendroidal setting.

5
The coherent nerve

In order to define a list coherent nerve functor
{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}:\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}\to\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
, we construct an appropriate functor
\mathbb{V}:\Upsilon\to\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
and proceed by Yoneda extension to obtain a
rigidification
functor
\mathfrak{O}:\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}\to\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
which has as right adjoint
{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}
. Taking our cue from the pattern developed in the ordinary nerve construction, we define simplicial operads
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Upsilon
which, not only are cofibrant replacements of the operads
T_{\alpha}
, but are also simplicially well-behaved.

5.1
Fundamental simplicial objects in
\mathsf{sList}

We begin with a preliminary discussion. Let
\mathscr{Q}
be a simplicial operad.
The monoidal envelope
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
inherits a simplicial enrichment from
\mathscr{Q}
(see Remark
2.4
).
An
n
-simplex in the coherent nerve, say
x\in N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{n}
, is represented by a simplicially enriched functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}}

where
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}
is the ordinary simplicial thickening of the category
\Delta^{n}
(see Appendix
B
).

Consider the associated discrete simplicial objects
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{\bullet},\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet}:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{Cat}
. Recall that we have
(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{\bullet}\cong\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet})
, where
\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet}:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{Op}
is the discrete simplicial object associated to
\mathscr{Q}
(Remark
2.4
). Now, consider the morphism
x_{\bullet}:\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{\bullet}\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet}
associated to
x
.

By applying the nerve functor
N:\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{sSet}
dimension-wise, and using
N\mathscr{L}\cong\mathscr{L}N^{l}
(Proposition
3.5
), we may regard
x_{\bullet}
as a morphism
N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{\bullet})\to\mathscr{L}N^{l}(\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet})
in
\mathsf{sSet}^{\Delta^{op}}
. Equivalently,
x_{\bullet}
is a dimension-wise listing

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\bullet}:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{\bullet})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N^{l}(\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

in
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}^{\Delta^{op}}
.
We may apply the perfect-function factorization dimension-wise to obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}}_{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{l}(\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{x}}

for some simplicial object
\mathbb{U}_{x}:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
.

We see that the simplex
x
is represented functionally by
f_{x}
. The list nerve functor
N^{l}
is right adjoint to the free operad functor
\tau_{1}:\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}\to\mathsf{Op}
. Thus,
f_{x}
can be transposed into a morphism
g_{x}:\mathbb{V}_{x}\to\mathscr{Q}_{\bullet}
in
\mathsf{Op}^{\Delta^{op}}
, where
\mathbb{V}_{x}=\tau_{1}\mathbb{U}_{x}
(dimension-wise).

Finally, it is easy to see (say, from the proof of Proposition
4.1
) that
\mathbb{U}_{x}
has the following property: the simplicial lists
(\mathbb{U}_{x})_{m}
,
[m]\in\Delta
, have the same set of
0
-simplices and all simplicial operations are identity on
0
-simplices. Thus, the simplicial object
\mathbb{V}_{x}\in\mathsf{Op}^{\Delta^{op}}
is discrete (in the sense of Remark
2.4
) and may be regarded as a simplicial operad and
g_{x}:\mathbb{V}_{x}\to\mathscr{Q}
a morphism in
\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
.

Reverse engineering the above steps, we may conclude the following:

For a simplicial operad
\mathscr{Q}
, simplices in the coherent nerve
N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{n}
,
[n]\in\Delta
, are classified by morphism of simplicial operads
\mathbb{V}\to\mathscr{Q}
for simplicial operads
\mathbb{V}
which are freely generated by simplicial objects
\mathbb{U}:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
which are indexed by a perfect listing with domain
N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{\bullet})
.

In what follows, we classify the aforementioned simplicial objects
\mathbb{U}
. As expected, such objects are indexed by simplices in the nerve of the category
\Delta_{+}
.

Construction 5.1

(Thick version of
U_{\alpha}
)
.

Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
. We adopt the usual notation. For
[k],[m]\in\Delta
, define the set

\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}[k,m]=\coprod_{\theta:\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m}}A_{\theta(k)}.

The mapping
[k,m]\mapsto\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}[k,m]
is functionally simplicial in the variable
m
and listing simplicial in the variable
k
(in the same manner
U_{\alpha}
is, as described in Construction
4.1
).

This way, we may regard
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
as a simplicial object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[m\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}[-,m]}

For ease, we will denote
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}^{(m)}=\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}[-,m]
for all
m\geq 0
.

Notice that for all
[m]\in\Delta
we have

\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}[0,m]\cong\coprod_{i\in[n]}A_{i}

and moreover, the simplicial operations are identities on the latter set. Hence, applying the free operad functor produces a simplicially enriched operad which we denote

\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}=\tau_{1}\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}.

Remark 5.1
.

\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
may be regarded to be obtained by freely resolving the partial level-wise composition operation in
U_{\alpha}
discussed in Note
Note
.

Proposition 5.1
.

For all
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
, there is an augmentation
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}\dashrightarrow U_{\alpha}
with extra degeneracies.

Proof.

The simplicial category
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}
, by virtue of being a free resolution, is equipped with an augmentation
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}\dashrightarrow\Delta^{n}
with extra degeneracies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}}

This induces an augmentation with extra degeneracies
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}\dashrightarrow U_{\alpha}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}^{(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}^{(0)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}}

The morphism
d_{0}:\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}^{(0)}\to U_{\alpha}
is defined, for
\theta:\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{0}
and
a\in A_{\theta(k)}
, by

d_{0}(\theta,a)=(d_{0}(\theta),a)

where
d_{0}(\theta)
is the composite of
\theta
with the augmentation map
d_{0}:\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{0}\to\Delta^{n}
. Similarly, the extra degeneracies
s_{-1}:\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}^{(m-1)}\to\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}^{(m)}
are defined, for
\theta:\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m-1}
and
a\in A_{\theta(k)}
by

s_{-1}(\theta,a)=(s_{-1}(\theta),a)

where
s_{-1}(\theta)
is the composite of
\theta
with the extra degeneracy
s_{-1}:\mathbb{\Delta}^{m-1}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{m}
pertaining to the resolution
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}
.
∎

Corollary 5.1
.

There is a weak equivalence of simplicial operads
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}\to T_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
.

Proof.

By applying the fundamental operad functor
\tau_{1}
to
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}\dashrightarrow U_{\alpha}
, we obtain an augmentation with extra degeneracies
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}\dashrightarrow T_{\alpha}
. The induced map
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}\to T_{\alpha}
is identity on objects by construction. In particular, given any sequence of colors
{\underline{a}}
and color
b
in
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
, we have an augmentation with extra degeneracies
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}({\underline{a}},b)\dashrightarrow T_{\alpha}({\underline{a}},b)
from which we conclude that the induced map
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}({\underline{a}},b)\rightarrow T_{\alpha}({\underline{a}},b)
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets. Hence the conclusion.
∎

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{U}:\Delta^{op}\to\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
be a simplicial object. Then, there exists a pointwise perfect listing
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}\mathbb{U}
if and only if
\mathbb{U}\cong\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
.
We may define a listing

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\alpha}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta:\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\theta,a))_{a\in A_{\theta(k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

for all
k,m\geq 0
. This listing is perfect by construction of
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
.

For the converse, let
\mathbb{U}\in\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}^{\Delta^{op}}
and let
\eta:\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}{\mathrel{\to}}\mathbb{U}
be a perfect listing. We write
\mathbb{U}[k,m]
for the
k
-simplices of the simplicial list
\mathbb{U}_{m}
. This way, for all
k,m\geq 0
we have a perfect listing of sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{k,m}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m})_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}[k,m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

For
\theta:\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m}
, let us denote
\eta_{k,m}(\theta):A_{\theta}\to\mathbb{U}[k,m]
, where
A_{\theta}\in\Delta_{+}
. Since
\eta
is perfect, we have an isomorphism

\mathbb{U}[k,m]\cong\coprod_{\theta:\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m}}A_{\theta}.

Consider the case
k=0
. Let
A_{i}
be the ordered set corresponding as above to the morphism
\{i\}:\Delta^{0}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{0}
which picks the object
i\in\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{0}
. In general, let
A_{i}^{(m)}
denote the ordered set corresponding to the morphism
\{i\}:\Delta^{0}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m}
. Consider the terminal map
s:[m]\to[0]
in
\Delta
. We have a commutative square in
\mathsf{List}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{0})_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{U}[0,0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m})_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{U}[0,m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}}

The listings on the top and bottom are perfect. By restricting to elements
\{i\}:\Delta^{0}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{0}
we see that there is an isomorphism
A_{i}\cong A_{i}^{(m)}
for all
i
and for all
[m]\in\Delta
.

For general
k
, consider the morphism
t_{k}:[0]\to[k]
,
0\mapsto k
, in
\Delta
. We have a commutative square in
\mathsf{List}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m})_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{U}[k,m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m})_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{U}[0,m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{k}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{k,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{k}^{*}}

Again, the horizontal listings are perfect. The map on the right is a function since
\mathbb{U}_{m}
is assumed to be operadic. By restricting to some element
\theta:\Delta^{k}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m}
in
N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{m})_{k}
, we see that we must have an isomorphism
A_{\theta}\cong A_{\theta(k)}^{(m)}
. Combining with the previous result, we conclude that
A_{\theta}\cong A_{\theta(k)}
for all
\theta
.

By looking at the spine of
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}
it is not difficult to recover morphisms
\alpha_{i}:A_{i-1}\to A_{i}
so that we form a simplex
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
with
\alpha(i)=A_{i}
. It is also easy to check that the simplicial operations of
\mathbb{U}
agree with those of
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
.

∎

The above classification has immediate important consequences.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
\mathscr{Q}\in\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
. There is a natural bijection

N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{n}\cong\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}}\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}(\mathbb{V}_{\alpha},\mathscr{Q})

for all
[n]\in\Delta
.

Proof.

This follows by the discussion in the beginning of this section.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

For all
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
, the simplicial operad
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
is cofibrant.

Proof.

Consider a lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

in the category of simplicial operads, where the map on the right is a trivial fibration. By the previous result, we may transpose this lifting problem into the category of simplicial categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}\mathscr{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The morphism on the right is still a trivial fibration, since the functor
\mathscr{L}
preserves trivial fibrations (Lemma
2.4
). Hence, since
\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}
is a cofibrant simplicial category, this lifting problem has a solution. By commutativity, any chosen lift has to be of shape
\alpha
. Therefore, the original lifting problem has a solution.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

For all
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})
,
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
is a cofibrant replacement of
T_{\alpha}
in the category of simplicial operads.

Remark 5.2
.

To justify the title of this section, we can define a
fundamental
simplicial object in
\mathsf{sList}
, say
\mathbb{U}
, to be equipped with a perfect listing
u:N(\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{\bullet}){\mathrel{\to}}\mathbb{U}
for some
n\geq 0
and such that the sequence
u(n)
is a singleton. Then, Theorem
5.1
says that fundamental simplicial objects in
\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
are of the form
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
for rooted simplices
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
.

5.2
The homotopy coherent list nerve

It is not difficult to see that
\alpha\mapsto\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
yields a functor
\mathbb{V}:\Upsilon\to\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
. By Yoneda extension, we obtain a
list rigidification
functor
\mathfrak{O}:\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}\to\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
. We define the
list coherent nerve functor

{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}:\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}\to\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
to be the right adjoint of
\mathfrak{O}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mapsto\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{O}}

For
\mathscr{Q}\in\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
we will have

({N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q})_{n}=\coprod_{\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}}\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}(\mathbb{V}_{\alpha},\mathscr{Q}).

Proposition 5.2
.

The following squares of categories and functors commute (up to natural isomorphism)

\hbox to114.69pt{\vbox to55.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.3457pt\lower-27.4479pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
