\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Proof.

The first square says that our coherent nerve for simplicial operads extends the coherent nerve of simplicial categories. This is clear. To verify the commutativity of the second square, let

\mathscr{Q}\in\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
. By Proposition
5.2
, simplices in the set
N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{n}
are in bijection with morphisms
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{Q}
for
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
, for all
[n]\in\Delta
. Each particular
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
has a canonical rooted decomposition as

\alpha=\coprod_{a\in A_{n}}\alpha_{a}

Given that
\alpha\mapsto\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
respects disjoint union (this is easy to see), each morphism
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{Q}
determines and is determined by a sequence of simplices in
{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q}
, namely the sequence
(\mathbb{V}_{\alpha_{a}}\to\mathscr{Q})_{a\in A_{n}}
. Hence the conclusion.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

We could have used the mapping
\alpha\mapsto FU_{*}T_{\alpha}
in order to obtain a coherent nerve. While
FU_{*}T_{\alpha}
is a cofibrant replacement of
T_{\alpha}
, it has too many simplices in the mapping spaces and does not give the precise results we want.

Theorem 5.2
.

If
\mathscr{Q}\in\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}
is enriched in Kan complexes, then
{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q}
is a quasi-operad.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.2
, we have
\mathscr{L}({N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q})\cong N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})
. In case the mapping spaces in
Q
are Kan complexes, then so are the mapping spaces in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
and hence
N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})
is an
\infty
-category. Thus,
{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q}
is an
\infty
-operad.
∎

Example 5.1

(The
\infty
-operads of spaces and
\infty
-categories)
.

Let
\mathsf{Kan}_{\Delta}
denote the simplicially enriched category of Kan complexes. We may form the simplicial operad
\mathsf{Kan}_{\Delta}^{\times}
via the cartesian product. Since the spaces of operations are Kan complexes, we obtain an
\infty
-operad

\mathscr{S}^{\times}={N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}(\mathsf{Kan}_{\Delta}^{\times})

which may be regarded as the
\infty
-operad of spaces.

Similarly, let
\mathsf{qCat}_{\Delta}
be the simplicially enriched category of
\infty
-categories (quasi-categories). Since the mapping spaces are not Kan complexes, we consider
\mathsf{qCat}_{\Delta}^{\circ}
, which is the simplicial subcategory with mapping spaces the maximal Kan complexes in the mapping spaces of
\mathsf{qCat}_{\Delta}
. In similar fashion, we may form the simplicial operad
(\mathsf{qCat}_{\Delta}^{\circ})^{\times}
via the cartesian product and apply the coherent nerve functor to obtain an
\infty
-operad of
\infty
-categories

\mathsf{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times}={N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}(\mathsf{qCat}_{\Delta}^{\circ})^{\times}

5.3
Some properties of rigidification

Rigidification is usually hard to understand. The reason is that, for
X\in\mathsf{sList}_{\text{Op}}
, the simplicial operad
\mathfrak{O}X
is given as a colimit of simplicial operads and the latter are not easy to compute. In case
X
is a simplicial set, even though the rigidification
\mathfrak{C}X
is a colimit of simplicial categories, we do know something about the shape of the mapping spaces via the necklace theorem of Dugger and Spivak (
[
16
]
, see also
[
22
]
for a related discussion on colimits of categories). This provides us a little window into understanding some facts about
\mathfrak{O}X
, at least for the case where
X=N^{l}(\mathscr{P})
for some operad
\mathscr{P}
, in terms of the simplicial category
\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad and let us denote by
\mathfrak{O}\mathscr{P}
the list rigidification of
N^{l}\mathscr{P}
. We can describe the simplicial category
\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})
as follows. Its objects are those of
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
. Given two sequences
{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}\in\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}
, a
k
-simplex (or
k
-morphism) in the mapping space
\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})({\underline{a}},{\underline{b}})_{k}
is represented by a pair
(f,B)
consisting of:

-

A simplex
f:\Delta^{n}\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}_{{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}}
, where notation indicates that
0\mapsto{\underline{a}}
and
n\mapsto{\underline{b}}
.

-

A
k
-morphism
B\in\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}(0,n)_{k}
(which is just a bracketing formula, see Appendix
B
).

We identify two such pairs
(f:\Delta^{n}\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}_{{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}},B)
and
(g:\Delta^{m}\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}_{{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}},B^{\prime})
if there is a simplicial operation
\sigma:[m]\to[n]
such that
\sigma^{*}f=g

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}_{{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

and
B^{\prime}\mapsto B
under the induces map
\mathbb{\Delta}^{m}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}
.

On the other hand, by Proposition
3.4
, the first piece of data above is just a morphism of operads

f:T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P}_{{\underline{a}},{\underline{b}}}

for some
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}
and such that
f(A_{0})={\underline{a}}
and
f(A_{n})={\underline{b}}
.
By rooted decomposition of simplices in
\Delta_{+}
, we obtain a decomposition functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f:T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P},B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((f_{a}:T_{\alpha_{a}}\to\mathscr{P},B))_{a\in A_{n}}}

This is well defined, since the relation we have on representatives is preserved by rooted decomposition.

Definition 5.1

(Sequenced morphism)
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad and
\mathscr{Q}
be a simplicial operad. We say that a morphism of simplicial categories

\phi:\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}

is
sequenced
if for all pairs
(f:T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P},B)
representing a morphism in
\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})
we have that:

-

\phi
preserves the above decomposition, meaning that we have

\phi(f:T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P},B)=(\phi(f_{a}:T_{\alpha_{a}}\to\mathscr{P},B))_{a\in A_{n}}

-

In case
\alpha
is rooted,
\phi(f:T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P},B)
is a singleton sequence as a morphism in
\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
.

Construction 5.2

(The universal sequenced morphism)
.

For an operad
\mathscr{P}
, we have an identity on colors morphism

S_{\mathscr{P}}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})

as follows. Given a
k
-morphism
(f,B)\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})({\underline{a}},b)_{k}
, for some sequence of colors
{\underline{a}}
and color
b
, represented by a pair
f:T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P}_{{\underline{a}},b}
for some rooted
\alpha\in N(\Delta_{+})_{n}^{root}
and
B:0\to n
in
\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{k}
, we let
S_{\mathscr{P}}(f,B)
be the operation in
\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{P})
which is the image under the structure map
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}\to\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})

of the operation
(B,r_{\alpha}):A_{0}\to A_{n}
. The latter operation lives in
\mathbb{U}_{\alpha}
, when regarding
B
as a morphism
\Delta^{1}\to\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}_{k}
. Then, we extend
S_{\mathscr{P}}
sequentially, so that it is a sequenced morphism by construction.

Equivalently, we can define
S_{\mathscr{P}}
via a series of transpositions through adjunctions as follows. Consider the identity morphism
id:\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})
, which induces the unit
N^{l}(\mathscr{P})\to{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}(\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P}))
in
\mathsf{sList}
. By applying the functor
\mathscr{L}
, which commutes with both nerves, we obtain a morphism
N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P}))
in
\mathsf{sSet}
. The latter transposes into a morphism
S_{\mathscr{P}}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})
in
\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}
by adjunction.

Lemma 5.1

(Universal property of
S_{\mathscr{P}}
)
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad and
\mathscr{Q}
be a simplicial operad. Given a sequenced morphism
\phi:\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}
, there is a unique morphism of simplicial operads
\psi:\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{Q}
such that
(\mathscr{L}\psi)\circ S_{\mathscr{P}}=\phi
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\mathscr{L}\psi}

Proof.

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}(\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P}),\mathscr{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{sList}(N\mathscr{P},N_{\Delta}\mathscr{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P}),\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{mon}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{sSet}(N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}),N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}))_{mon}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}),\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{seq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
\mathsf{Op}_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P}),\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{mon}
and
\mathsf{sSet}(N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}),N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}))_{mon}
are the sets of morphisms which respect monoid structure, and

\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}),\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})_{seq}
is the set of sequenced morphisms. The unlabelled isomorphisms in the top square are the maps given by adjunction. Notice that the vertical maps induced by applying
\mathscr{L}
are also isomorphisms.

For the triangle on the bottom, it is evident by scrutiny that the restriction of the adjunction isomorphism
\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}),\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{sSet}(N(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}),N_{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}))
to sequenced morphisms is a bijection into maps of monoids. Hence, the function
S_{\mathscr{P}}^{*}
induced by composing with
S_{\mathscr{P}}
is also a bijection.
∎

Proposition 5.3
.

For all operads
\mathscr{P}
, the simplicial operad
\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})
is cofibrant.

Proof.

Consider a lifting problem in the category of simplicial operads

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the map on the right is a trivial fibration. By, applying the lists functor
\mathscr{L}
, and in light the universal property of
\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})
, consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}\mathscr{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}

The map on the right is a trivial fibration of simplicial categories since the functor
\mathscr{L}
preserves trivial fibrations (Lemma
2.4
), hence there exists a lift
\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}\mathscr{K}
since
\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})
is cofibrant. Moreover, this lift has to be sequenced because of commutativity. Hence, we obtain the desired lift
\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{K}
.

∎

Remark 5.4

(Cofibrant replacement)
.

By construction, we have the colimit formula
\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})\cong\displaystyle\colim_{T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P}}\mathbb{V}_{\alpha}
. Since
\mathscr{P}\cong\tau_{1}(N^{l}\mathscr{P})
, we also have the colimit formula
\mathscr{P}\cong\displaystyle\colim_{T_{\alpha}\to\mathscr{P}}T_{\alpha}
. Thus, we obtain a structure map
\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{P}
, where
\mathscr{P}
is regarded as a discrete simplicial operad.

In a previous version of this paper, we
erroneously
claimed that this map is weak equivalence, so that
\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})
serves as a cofibrant replacement for
\mathscr{P}
. We believe the result is true, but we leave it for subsequent work.

Notive that
\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{P}
being a weka equivalence would imply that the the universal sequenced morphisms
S_{\mathscr{P}}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})
is also a weak equivalence since we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}\mathfrak{O}(\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

In the proof of Proposition
3.3
we constructed a natural weak equivalence
D:\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})\to\mathscr{L}(FU_{*}\mathscr{P})
. By manner of construction,
D
is sequenced, hence it induces a morphism
R:\mathfrak{O}\mathscr{P}\to FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
. The map
\mathscr{L}R
fits into the following commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{C}(\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{O}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{L}(FU_{*}\mathscr{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathscr{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}R}

in which the two maps on the bottom are obtained by applying
\mathscr{L}
to the structure morphisms
\mathfrak{O}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}
and
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{P}
. The top triangle commutes by definition, while the bottom triangle commutes by inspection. Thus, we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{O}\mathscr{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FU_{*}\mathscr{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

which would imply that
R
is a weak equivalence as well.

Remark 5.5

(Homotopy coherence)
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be an operad and
\mathscr{Q}
be a simplicial operad. In Section
3.3
we discussed how it is sensible, in analogy with classical theory, to say that a homotopy coherent
\mathscr{Q}
-valued algebra of
\mathscr{P}
can be said to be a morphism of simplicial operads
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
.

In light of the coherent nerve we proposed earlier, it also makes sense for homotopy coherent algebras to be defined as morphisms
\mathfrak{O}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
.
The advantage of studying maps
\mathfrak{O}\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{Q}
is that they can be transposed into morphisms of simplicial lists
N^{l}\mathscr{P}\to{N_{\Delta}}^{\!\!\!\!l\;}\mathscr{Q}
.

The true benefit of such a transposition for coherence can only come to surface when more theory is developed. Nonetheless, we deem the ability to state that a homotopy coherent algebra is a map of simplicial lists an important simplification in the study of coherent phenomena. We illustrate with a couple of examples.

Example 5.2

(
A_{\infty}
-spaces and
A_{\infty}
-algebras)
.

We can model
A_{\infty}
-spaces as morphisms of simplicial lists
N^{l}\mathsf{Assoc}\to\mathscr{S}^{\times}
and
A_{\infty}
-algebras as morphisms of simplicial lists
N^{l}\mathsf{Assoc}\to\mathsf{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times}
, where
\mathscr{S}^{\times}
and
\mathsf{Cat}_{\infty}^{\times}
are the
\infty
-operads of spaces and
\infty
-categories constructed in Example
5.1
.

Remark 5.6
.

The above notions ought to correspond, via a good theory of fibrations, to monoidal
\infty
-groupoids and monoidal
\infty
-categories. We hope to achieve such results in the sequel papers.

Example 5.3

(Weak enrichment)
.

Let
S
be a set and
P
be an
\infty
-operad. We can say that a
P
-enriched category with set of objects
S
is a morphism of simplicial lists
N^{l}\mathsf{Hom}_{S}\to P
, where
\mathsf{Hom}_{S}
is the operad from Example

2.4
. In particular, categories weakly enriched in spaces give us a variant for
(\infty,1)
-categories, while enriching in
\infty
-categories produces a variant for
(\infty,2)
-categories.

6
Homology of simplicial lists

We briefly discuss a natural variant for the homology of a simplicial list. Other possible variants, and more sophisticated results about homology and its applications are left for future work.

Let
\mathsf{Ab}
denote the category of abelian groups. Let
\mathbb{Z}:\mathsf{List}\to\mathsf{Ab}
be the functor that sends an object
X
in
\mathsf{List}
to the free abelian group
\mathbb{Z}X
in
\mathsf{Ab}
and send a morphism
u:X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
in
\mathsf{List}
to the homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}(u):\mathbb{Z}X\to\mathbb{Z}Y
in
\mathsf{Ab}
that linearly extends the assignment

\mathbb{Z}(u)(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{|u(x)|}u(x)_{i}

for
x
in
X
where
u(x)
is the list
\left(u(x)_{1},u(x)_{2},\dots u(x)_{|u(x)|}\right)
. Here, note that if
u(x)=\emptyset
is the empty list then
\mathbb{Z}(u)(x)=0
.

Let
\mathsf{sAb}
denote the category of simplicial abelian groups,
\text{Ch}_{\geq 0}(\mathsf{Ab})
the category of nonnegatively graded chain complexes, and
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
the functor category from
\Delta^{op}
to
\mathsf{List}
, as discussed in
3.1
. Recall that
\mathsf{sList}
is a wide subcategory of
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
.
The functor
\mathbb{Z}:\mathsf{List}\to\mathsf{Ab}
induces a functor from
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}

to
\mathsf{sAb}
. Hence composing with the full alternating face map complex functor we get a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Ch}_{\geq 0}(\mathsf{Ab})}

Definition 6.1

(Homology)
.

Let
\mathsf{grAb}
be the category of graded abelian groups. Let
H:\text{Ch}_{\geq 0}(\mathsf{Ab})\to\mathsf{grAb}
denote the homology functor. For
X
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
, we define the homology
H(X)
of
X
as the homology
H(C(X))
of the chain complex
C(X)
.

Note that an augmented simplicial object
X\dashrightarrow X_{-1}
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
induces a morphism
X\to X_{-1}
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
where
X_{-1}
is considered as the constant simplicial list. Hence it induces a morphism of homology groups
H(X)\to H(X_{-1})
. Notice that we have
H(X_{-1})\cong\mathbb{Z}X_{-1}
where
\mathbb{Z}X_{-1}
denotes free abelian group generated by the set
X_{-1}
considered as a graded abelian group concentrated at degree
0
.

Theorem 6.1
.

If an augmented simplicial object
X\dashrightarrow X_{-1}
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
has extra-degeneracies, then the induces morphisms
H(X)\to\mathbb{Z}X_{-1}
is an isomorphism of graded abelian groups.

Proof.

The sequence of morphisms
\left\{h_{n}:C_{n}(X)\to C_{n+1}(X)\right\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
induced by the morphism
s_{-1}:X_{n}\to X_{n+1}
for
n\geq 0
gives a chain homotopy from the chain map
C(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C(d_{0})}}{{\to}}C(X_{-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C(s_{0})}}{{\to}}C(X)
to the identity chain map on
C(X)
. ∎

Notice that the empty set
\emptyset
is the initial and terminal object in
\mathsf{List}
. Hence the empty set
\emptyset
considered as a constant simplicial list is an initial and terminal object in
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
. Also note that the homology of the simplicial list
\emptyset
is the trivial abelian group
0
.

Proposition 6.1
.

H(N^{l}\mathsf{Assoc})\cong 0

Proof.

We have
N^{l}\mathsf{Assoc}\cong N(\Delta_{+})^{root}
. Hence, it is enough to show that the unique augmentation
N(\Delta_{+})^{root}\dashrightarrow\emptyset
has extra-degeneracies. Let
\alpha
be
A_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}}}{{\to}}A_{1}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{m}}}{{\to}}A_{m}
in
N(\Delta_{+})^{root}_{m}
. Define
s_{-1}(\alpha)
as
\emptyset\to A_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}}}{{\to}}A_{1}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{m}}}{{\to}}A_{m}
in
N(\Delta_{+})_{m+1}
. It is straight forward to check that this assignment gives the required extra degeneracy maps. Hence the result follows by Theorem
6.1
. ∎

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\alpha
be
A_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{1}}}{{\to}}A_{1}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{k}}}{{\to}}A_{k}
in
N(\Delta_{+})_{k}
. Then

H(N^{l}T_{\alpha})\cong\mathbb{Z}A_{0}

where
\mathbb{Z}A_{0}
is considered as the graded abelian group concentrated at degree
0
.

Proof.

Let
d_{0}
be the morphism from
(N^{l}T_{\alpha})_{0}=\displaystyle\coprod_{i=0}^{k}A_{i}
to
A_{0}
that sends
x
in
A_{i}
to
\alpha_{0,i}^{-1}(x)
considered as list of elements in
A_{0}
. Let
\beta
be depicted by
B_{0}\to B_{1}
. Then for any operad morphism
f:T_{\beta}\to T_{\alpha}
in
(N^{l}T_{\alpha})_{1}
we have
d_{0}d_{0}(f)=\alpha_{0,i}^{-1}(f(B_{1}))=d_{0}d_{1}(f)
. Hence we have an augmentation

N^{l}T_{\alpha}\dashrightarrow A_{0}.

Now we construct extra degeneracies for this augmentation. Define
s_{-1}:A_{0}\to(N^{l}T_{\alpha})_{0}
as the inclusion. Then clearly
d_{0}s_{-1}
is identity on
A_{0}
. Let
\beta
be
B_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta_{1}}}{{\to}}B_{1}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta_{s}}}{{\to}}B_{s}
in
N(\Delta_{+})_{s}
and let
f
be an operad morphism from
T_{\beta}
to
T_{\alpha}
. This means
f
is an
s
-simplex in
N^{l}T_{\alpha}
. Then we define
\gamma
depicted by
C_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{1}}}{{\to}}C_{1}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{s+1}}}{{\to}}C_{s+1}
in
N(\Delta_{+})_{s+1}
as follows:
C_{i+1}=C_{i}
for
i\geq 0
and

C_{0}=\displaystyle\coprod_{b\in B_{0}}\alpha_{0,j(f(b))}^{-1}(f(b))

where
j(b)
is determined by saying
f(b)\in A_{j(f(b))}
and
\gamma_{i+1}=\beta_{i}
for
i\geq 1
and
\gamma_{1}:C_{0}\to C_{1}
be given by
\gamma_{1}(x)=b
if
x\in\alpha_{0,j(f(b))}^{-1}(f(b))
.
Note that
T_{\beta}
is a suboperad of
T_{\gamma}
. We extend
f

to an operad morphism
s_{-1}(f)
from
T_{\gamma}
to
T_{\alpha}

by sending
p_{0,1}^{(b)}
to
p_{0,j(f(b))}^{(f(b))}
for
b
in
C_{1}=B_{0}
. It is straight forward to see that
d_{i}s_{-1}=s_{-1}d_{i-1}
for
i>0
and
s_{i}s_{-1}=s_{-1}s_{i-1}
for
i>0
. Hence the result follows by Theorem
6.1
.
∎

Let
\mathsf{List}^{2}
denote the category whose objects are pairs of sets
(X,Y)
where
Y
is a subset of
X
and morphisms from
(X,Y)
to
(W,Z)
are morphisms
f:X\to Z
in
\mathsf{List}
which sends elements in
Y
to lists in
Z
. Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}^{2}}
denote simplicial objects in
\mathsf{List}^{2}
. Let
Q:\mathsf{List}^{2}\to\mathsf{List}
denote the functor that sends a pair
(X,Y)
to the set difference
X-Y
and a morphism
f:(X,Y)\to(W,Z)
in
\mathsf{List}^{2}
to the morphism
Q(f):(X-Y)\to(W-Z)
where
Q(f)(x)
is the list obtained from
f(x)
by deleting every element in
Z
. Let
\iota:\mathsf{List}\to\mathsf{List}^{2}
be the functor that sends
X
to
(X,\emptyset)
and morphisms to itself. The functors
Q
,
\iota
respectively induce the functors
Q:\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}^{2}}\to\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}
and
\iota:\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}}\to\widetilde{\mathsf{sList}^{2}}

Let
Y
be a subsimplicial list of a simplicial list
X
. We define the relative homology groups as follows:

H(X,Y)=H(C(X)/C(Y)).

Then the short exact sequence

0\to C(X)\to C(Y)\to C(X)/C(Y)\to 0

induces the following long exact

\cdots\to H_{n+1}(Y)\to H_{n+1}(X)\to H_{n+1}(X,Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{n}}}{{\to}}H_{n}(Y)\to H_{n}(X)\to\cdots

where
\delta_{n}
is the
n
th connecting homomorphism for
n\geq 0
. This long exact sequence of homology groups will be called the
homology long exact sequence
associated to the pair
(X,Y)
.

Assume that
Y
is a subsimplicial list of a simplicial list
X
. Then it is straightforward to prove that we have

H(X,Y)\cong H(Q(X,Y)).

Proposition 6.2
.

If
\alpha
is in
N(\Delta_{+})_{k}
then we have an isomorphism

H(U_{\alpha})\cong H(N^{l}T_{\alpha}).

induced by the inclusion
U_{\alpha}\to N^{l}T_{\alpha}
which picks the
\alpha
-shaped simplex
1_{T_{\alpha}}:T_{\alpha}\to T_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let
s_{-1}
be the extra degeneracies on the augmentation
N^{l}T_{\alpha}\dashrightarrow A_{0}
constructed in the proof of Theorem
6.2
. These extra degeneracies restrict to extra degeneracies on the augmentation
Q(N^{l}T_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})\dashrightarrow\emptyset
. Hence we are done by considering the long exact homology sequence for the pair
(N^{l}T_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})
.
∎

Remark 6.1
.

The homology of dendroidal sets has been studied in
[
4
]
.
We do not study functors relating dendroidal sets and simplicial lists, so we cannot give a complete comparison between the homology defined in this section and the homology defined in
[
4
]
. However, we can say that they both generalize the singular homology of simplicial sets, where a simplicial set is considered as a dendroidal set supported on the linear trees and considered as a simplicial list whose all degeneracy and face maps are functions.

Moreover, considering the list nerve of a non-symmetric operad and the dendroidal nerve of its symmetrization, the two homologies agree on some operads like the one obtained from a planar tree and the associative operad. For this, compare Proposition
6.1
and Theorem
6.2
in this section with Theorem 6.5 and Corollary 5.13 in
[
4
]
.

Another variant for dendroidal homology has been studied in
[
27
]
. Again, there seems to be a point of comparison between Proposition
6.1
and
[
27
, Example 3.2]
.

Appendix A
Augmentations and extra degeneracies

Definition A.1

(Augmented simplicial set)
.

An augmented simplicial set is a functor
X:\Delta_{+}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
.

The augmented simplex category
\Delta_{+}
is the category of finite ordinals in which the empty ordinal
[-1]
is included. Hence, an augmented simplicial set is determined by specifying:

-

A simplicial set
X
.

-

A set
X_{-1}
which is equipped with a map
d_{0}:X_{0}\to X_{-1}
such that
d_{0}d_{0}=d_{1}d_{0}
.

In other words, we regard augmentation as extra structure on a simplicial set and use the notation
X\dashrightarrow X_{-1}
to indicate data as above. Notice that we do have an actual map of simplicial sets
X\to X_{-1}
into the constant simplicial object which maps a simplices of
X
to the face
d_{0}
of one of its vertices (this is well-defined by the above identity).

Definition A.2

(Extra degeneracies,
[
19
, p.200]
)
.

An augmented simplicial set
X\dashrightarrow X_{-1}
is said to have extra degeneracies in case there exist maps
s_{-1}:X_{n-1}\to X_{n}
,
n\geq 0
, which satisfy
d_{0}s_{-1}=1
and for all
i,j\geq 0
,
d_{i+1}s_{-1}=s_{-1}d_{i}
and
s_{j+1}s_{-1}=s_{-1}s_{j}
.

Lemma A.1

(
[
19
, Lemma 5.1]
)
.

If an augmented simplicial set
X\dashrightarrow X_{-1}
has extra-degeneracies, then the map
X\to X_{-1}
is a homotopy equivalence.

The idea behind the above lemma can be sketched as follows. Let us depict the
0
-simplices
X_{0}
which are degeneracies of elements of
X_{-1}
as generic points
\bullet
and see how the extra degeneracies do provide a recipe of contraction for simplices of
X
to bullet points. For a
0
-simplex
x\in X_{0}
, the degeneracy
s_{-1}x
has to be of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}x}

and thus provides a path for
x
to “slide back” into
\bullet
. Given a
1
-simplex
x_{0}\xrightarrow{\sigma}x_{1}
in
X_{1}
, the degeneracy
s_{-1}\sigma
is of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}x_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}x_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}\sigma}

and thus provides a path to slide the whole simplex back into
\bullet
. And so on for higher simplices. Natural examples of augmentations with extra degeneracies arise from free resolutions, where the term extra degeneracy makes good sense.

Remark A.1
.

The notion of augmentation and extra degeneracy applies to simplicial objects in any category. A statement which vastly generalizes the above lemma can be found in
[
38
, Corollary 4.5.2]
. A detailed account of extra degeneracies and variants of this notion can be found in
[
2
]
. In the context of
\infty
-categories, they appear as
split simplicial objects
in
[
34
, Definition 10.2.6.3]
.

Appendix B
Free resolutions

The idea of a free resolution is simple and follows the same pattern in various algebraic structures. Informally, by algebraic structure we mean any mathematical structure which has incorporated in it some sort of operation that allows to write equations of the form
A\cdot B=C
. To resolve
\cdot
involves forgetting the algebraic identities in the structure, and thus, in many examples a free-forgetful adjunction appears to control resolutions.

For pedagogical purposes, we first consider the free-forgetful adjunction between monoids and pointed sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Monoid}:U}

For a monoid
M
, the pointed set
UM
is simply the underlying set of
M
pointed by the unit element
1\in M
. For a pointed set
(X,e)
, the monoid
F(X,e)
is the monoid whose elements are words in
X
modulo a relation which says that
e
is the unit for the operation given by concatenation. More precisely, for all words
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
, we identify

(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\equiv(x_{1},\dots,e,\dots,x_{k})

This adjunction induces a functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FU_{*}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Monoid}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Monoid}^{\Delta^{op}}}

from monoids to simplicial monoids. For a monoid
M
, the monoid of
m
-simplices of
FU_{*}M
is the monoid
FU^{(m+1)}M
obtained by iterating the endofunctor
FU

(m+1)
-times. The simplicial structure follows formally by applications of the unit and counit of the adjunction and it is equipped with an augmentation
FU_{*}M\dashrightarrow M
with extra degeneracies.
We are interested in a heuristic unpacking in terms of
bracketing
.

Let
M
be a monoid. An element in
FUM
is just a word in
M
(technically a class, but we neglect the unit as it can always be removed). The augmentation morphism is given by the counit of the adjunction

\hbox to171.87pt{\vbox to33.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.93211pt\lower-16.54167pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.51 -10.91 L 36.2 -10.91}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{3.16216pt}{-7.88194pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 4.38 -10.91)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.27 L 0 -2.27}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{26.36217pt}{-7.88194pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 36.48 -10.91)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

while the extra degeneracy
s_{-1}
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x)}

Note how this really feels like a degeneracy operation.
This iteration of the resolution resolves multiplication in
M
.

The next bit of resolution (and extra degeneracy)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUFUM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FUM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{-1}}

can be described as follows. An element of the monoid
FUFUM
is a word of words in
M
, say

((x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}))

which we think of as a
bracketing
of the sequence
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})
. Then, the map
d_{1}
removes inner brackets, for instance

d_{1}((x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}))=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})

The map
d_{0}
applies multiplication inside the inner brackets and then removes them, for instance

d_{0}((x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}))=(x_{2}\cdot x_{1},x_{5}\cdot x_{4}\cdot x_{3})

The degeneracy
s_{0}
adds a layer of inner brackets

s_{0}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})=((x_{1}),(x_{2}),(x_{3}),(x_{4}),(x_{5}))

The extra-degeneracy
s_{-1}
adds brackets on the exterior

s_{-1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5})=((x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}))

Again, this feels like a degenerate thing to do.

In general, the elements of
FU^{(m)}M
are sequences with
m
-layers of brackets. The faces operations in the simplicial structure remove layers, except for the face
d_{0}
which applies multiplication in the inner brackets before removing them. The degeneracies add extra brackets around the layers. The extra degeneracy adds a pair of brackets outwardly.

In case we want to be more rigorous about what we mean by bracketing, we would say the following:

-

A word in
M
is the choice of an element
x
together with a factorization
x=x_{k}\cdot\dots\cdot x_{1}
of
x
.

-

A bracketed word is the choice of a factorization for each of the factors
x_{i}
.

-

And so on.

From this perspective, the extra degeneracies are the choice of the trivial factorization.

When applied to the free-forgetful adjunction between abelian groups and pointed sets, we obtain a simplicial abelian group which, under the Dold-Kan correspondence, provides the free resolution of the abelian group in the usual sense of chain complexes. The quasi-isomorphism to the original group is precisely the presence of the augmentation with extra degeneracies. In fact, the backward pointing morphisms involved in the definition of null-homotopy for chain complexes can be seen to derive by the extra degeneracies.
Another classical example is the free resolution for categories obtained by the free-forgetful adjunction between graphs and categories.

Definition B.1

(Graph)
.

A (reflexive, directed)
graph

G
is a
1
-truncated simplicial set, i.e. it consists of a set of vertices
G_{0}
and a set of edges
G_{1}
related by faces and degeneracy operations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}

which satisfy
d_{0}s_{0}=d_{1}s_{0}
.

A morphism of graphs is a natural transformation. We denote by
\mathsf{Graph}
the resulting category.

The evident adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Graph}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}:U}

assigns to a graph
G
the category
FG
which has as objects the vertex set
G_{0}
and as morphisms chains of “composable” edges in
G_{1}
(modulo a relation which says that degeneracies serve as identities in
FG
). For a category
\mathscr{C}
, the graph
U\mathscr{C}
has the objects of
\mathscr{C}
as vertices and morphisms of
\mathscr{C}
as edges, with identities being the degeneracies.

The free resolution
FU_{*}\mathscr{C}
, by construction, resolves the composition operation in
\mathscr{C}
. The simplicial structure of this resolution can be put in the exact same terms as the resolution for monoid described above, except that words are replaced by sequences of composable morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
. Moreover, given that
FU^{(m)}\mathscr{C}
has always the same objects of
\mathscr{C}
and that the simplicial operations are identity on objects, we may regard
FU_{*}\mathscr{C}
as a simplicially enriched category, which means we have
m
-simplices in the mapping spaces provided by morphism sets in the resolution.

By the augmentation and extra degeneracies, we do get for free that for all objects
a,b\in\mathscr{C}
we have a weak-equivalence of simplicial sets
(FU_{*}\mathscr{C})(a,b)\to\mathscr{C}(a,b)
, which means that the morphism
FU_{*}\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
is a weak equivalence
5
5

5

These are also called Dwyer-Kan equivalence, or DK-equivalences for short. They serve as weak equivalence in the Bergner model structure on simplicial categories (
[
6
]
).
of simplicial categories. In fact,
FU_{*}\mathscr{P}
serves as cofibrant replacement in the Bergner model structure (see
[
38
, Section 16.2]
for a proof).

More is true. It is an easy exercise to determine that there is an isomorphism
6
6

6

Sometimes this is taken to be a definition.

FU_{*}\Delta^{n}\cong\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}
, where
\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}
is the simplicial thickening of
\Delta^{n}
typically defined combinatorially as follows:

-

The objects are
0,\dots,n
.

-

An
m
-simplex in the mapping space
\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}(i,j)
is a chain of inclusions
U_{0}\subseteq\dots U_{m}
of subsets of
\{0,\dots,n\}
such that
i,j\in U_{0}
.

-

Faces and degeneracies are obtained by removing or repeating terms of chains of subsets as above.

-

Composition is given by union of sets.

The cosimplicial object
\Delta\to\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta},[n]\to\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}
, induces an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{C}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sSet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}_{\Delta}:N_{\Delta}}

between simplicial sets and simplicial categories, which is one of the fundamental constructions in homotopy theory as it establishes a Quillen equivalence between two models for
(\infty,1)
-categories. The functor
\mathfrak{C}
is also referred to as rigidification, since, when applied to an
\infty
-category it outputs a simplicial category where some compositions are strict. It is trickier to prove (for instance, by applying the Necklace Theorem of Dugger and Spivak proved in
[
16
]
, see Theorem 16.4.7 in
[
38
]
), but true, that we have an isomorphism

\mathfrak{C}(N\mathscr{C})\cong FU_{*}\mathscr{C}

for all categories
\mathscr{C}
.

Remark B.1
.

In full generality, given an adjunction
F:\mathscr{C}\leftrightarrows\mathscr{D}:G
, the comonad structure of
F\circ U
equips
(F\circ U)^{\circ(\bullet+1)}
with the structure of an augmented
simplicial object with extra degeneracies in the endofunctor category of
\mathscr{D}
.
